Continuation sheaves in dynamics: sheaf cohomology and bifurcation Thanks: The work of W.D.K. was partially supported by the Army Research Office under award W911NF1810306.
K. Alex Dowling, William D. Kalies, Robert C.A.M. Vandervorst

Abstract
Continuation of algebraic structures in families of dynamical systems is described using category theory, sheaves, and lattice algebras. Well-known concepts in dynamics, such as attractors or invariant sets, are formulated as functors on appropriate categories of dynamical systems mapping to categories of lattices, posets, rings or abelian groups. Sheaves are constructed from such functors, which encode data about the continuation of structure as system parameters vary. Similarly, morphisms for the sheaves in question arise from natural transformations. This framework is applied to a variety of lattice algebras and ring structures associated to dynamical systems, whose algebraic properties carry over to their respective sheaves. Furthermore, the cohomology of these sheaves are algebraic invariants which contain information about bifurcations of the parametrized dynamical systems.



∎

1
Introduction

Dynamical systems theory is the study of the structure of invariant sets, particularly those sets that govern the long-term behavior of a system.
As a dynamical system is perturbed, its global dynamics can change qualitatively through bifurcation.
The global dynamics of a system can be described by the structure of its attractors. Indeed, in his fundamental decomposition theorem, Conley
Conley
uses the set of all attractors in a system to establish a global decomposition into minimal (chain)-recurrent components and connecting orbits between them. The algebraic structure that underlies this decomposition is codified in the fact that the set of all attractors naturally forms a bounded, distributive lattice
KMV-1a
. In a series of papers
KMV-0
;
KMV-1a
;
KMV-1b
;
KMV-1c
;
KastiKV
;
GKV
the theoretical framework for such dynamically meaningful algebraic structures has been developed. This framework has been used to design algorithms to compute global dynamical information rigorously for explicit maps, cf.
database
.
To understand dependence on parameters in dynamical systems we develop
a natural mathematical framework to capture this variation.
For a parametrized family of dynamical systems, structures like the lattice of attractors at a fixed parameter value may vary dramatically under small perturbations. Indeed, bifurcations can occur on a Cantor set with positive measure in the parameter space.
Continuation relates these structures for varying parameter values.

Continuation of isolated invariant sets has been the central theme in Conley index theory. Conley
Conley
and Montgomery
Mont
, and later Salamon
Salamon
, formulate continuation in terms of the
space of isolated invariant sets
:

\mathrm{\mathrm{\Pi}}[{\mathsf{Isol}}]:=\Bigl\{(\phi,S)~\big|~\phi\text{ a flow on }X\text{ and }S\text{ an isolated invariant set for }\phi\Bigr\}.

Two pairs are ”close” in the space of isolated invariant sets if the flows are ”close” and the isolated invariant sets share a common isolating neighborhood. Two isolated invariant sets are related by continuation if they lie in the same quasicomponent of the space of isolated invariant sets. Both Montgomery and Salamon give proofs of the following crucial result:

Two invariant sets related by continuation share the same Conley index.

Montgomery proves this in the language of étalé spaces (sheaf theory).
By choosing an appropriate topology on
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Isol}}]
the map

(\phi,S){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\pi}}\phi,

where the flow
\phi
is an element of a topological space of flows on
X
, is a local homeomorphism.
With it, he constructs a long exact sequence of étalé spaces relating the cohomology of the invariant sets, the index of invariant sets, and the cohomology of some asymptotic sets.
Montgomory’s approach is not to be confused with sheaf cohomology.
By contrast, Salamon provides a direct flow-defined homotopy to establish the invariance of the Conley index. This avoids much of the algebraic topological machinery necessary for Montgomery’s approach. Salamon’s explicit method appeals to an analytic perspective of dynamics.
The latter approach does not suffice for understanding the continuation of algebraic structures such as lattices of attractors or Morse representations.

In
fran3
Franzosa extends the approach of Kurland for attractor-repeller pairs, cf.
Kurland
, to develop an intricate theory of continuation of Morse decompositions. The idea is to augment the concept of the space of isolated invariant sets to a space of “Morse decompositions” with an appropriate topology.
Our interest in data-driven dynamics, and the recent success of sheaf theory in topological data analysis, motivate us to revisit Montgomery’s viewpoint. We acknowledge this increased algebraic topology overhead with the confidence that this abstract formulation will yield a comprehensive computational framework for continuation.
The abstract formalism developed in this paper gives an elementary treatment of the continuation theory of Morse representations extending the theory by Franzosa which makes it applicable to techniques from sheaf theory such as sheaf cohomology.

In a series of papers, cf.
KMV-1a
;
KMV-1b
;
KMV-1c
, we developed an algebraic theory of attractors via distributive lattice theory. We use attractors as the starting point of our approach.

The first diagram [left], which was established in
(
KMV-1a
, Diag. (1))
, appears categorically in the second diagram [middle], and then in the associated étalé spaces [right].
For example, the space
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
is the space of points

(\phi,A)
, where
A\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
is an attractor. The latter is the analogue of the space of isolated invariant sets.
Diagram (
1
)[right] allows us to define a
sheaf of attractors
over the space of dynamical systems, cf. Fig.
1
.
In the first sections of the paper we develop the categorical theory of continuation for contravariant functors. We can apply this theory in various dynamical settings such as the lattice of attractors, the semi-lattice of isolated invariant sets, but also the (non-distributive) lattice of Morse representations.
For the latter
we adopt the algebraic treatment of Morse representations and decompositions introduced in
(
KMV-1c
, Def. 7)
, which repairs the classical approach of labeling of invariant sets.
Morse decompositions are formulated as an order embedding from a Morse representation into a finite poset. By applying the categorical theory to Morse representations and studying the resulting sheaves we obtain a generalization of
Franzosa’s theory of continuation of Morse decompositions.
In the various settings we can define associated sheaves, e.g. the sheaf of attractors and the sheaf of Morse representations. If we invoke sheaf cohomology the algebraic structures of attractors and Morse representations can be used to define new invariants. We investigate sheaf cohomology in the setting of bifurcation theory as an illustration.
With the strides made in computational dynamics
and the success of sheaf theory in topological data analysis (cellular sheaves), we have the necessary prerequisites to achieve a computational method for modeling continuation.

As a first step, the focus of this paper is to construct and study sheaves which encode the continuation of structures in dynamics. The first seven sections of the paper detail an abstract approach to building sheaves for an arbitrary structure. We routinely return to attractors to showcase how this approach may be applied.

Our first goal is to formulate algebraic structures in dynamics as functors. Section
2
equips dynamical systems with the compact-open topology, and then with a categorical structure using the notion of topological conjugacies. This yields the domain category for the aforementioned functors, and a topology to attach algebraic information to with sheaves.
Section
3
then explicitly shows that the attracting neighborhood lattice
{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
and the attractor lattice
{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
can be expressed as functors from the category of dynamical systems to a category of lattices. These constitute an example to which we apply the later theory.
Section
4
gives prerequisites for continuation: A category with a topology on the objects, and a pair of functors we call a ”continuation frame”. We prove the existence of an étalé space encoding continuation for these functors. The end of the section constructs morphisms of the étalé spaces using natural transformations between the corresponding functors.
Section
5
first applies the framework built in Section
4
to the attractor case from Section
3
. This yields the étalé space of attractors. Furthermore, we formulate a morphism of étalé spaces from the dual repeller operator, seen in Fig.
1
. Lastly, we describe the functorial setup for finite sublattices of attractors and Morse representations. This will eventually define a Morse representation sheaf.
While Section
4
produces étalé spaces for attractors, repellers, etc., in Section
6
we port over their algebra. We begin by equipping the attractor and repeller étalé spaces with the binary lattice operations. Then, the Conley form on attractors is stated on the level of the attractor étalé space. Later, when we discuss sheaf cohomology, the Conley form will be a crucial tool in building sheaves in an abelian category. The end of Section
6
expands on this, detailing how the algebra of attractors can be stored in a ring.
Section
7
passes through the equivalence between étalé spaces and sheaves.
From an abstract continuation frame we build a sheaf which encodes the continuation of the unstable structure.
The attractor functor begets an
attractor lattice sheaf
, and the Conley form becomes a morphism of sheaves. We also discuss the functors built at the end of Section
6
, which give us
{\mathbf{Ring}}
-valued sheaves storing the continuation of attractors. To conclude the section we consider the sheaf of finite attractor sublattices, and the sheaf of Morse representations, as set up in Section
5
.

Figure 1:
Given a parametrized dynamical system
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
, we construct an étalé space of attractors
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
over parameter space, cf. Sect.
10.1.2
. The fiber at a parameter value
\lambda\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
is the attractor lattice of
\phi_{\lambda}
. Global sections are illustrated by dotted lines. The failure of these global sections to reach all attractors can be measured using sheaf cohomology.

In the last three sections of the paper, we use the continuation sheaves to study bifurcations in parametrized dynamical systems.
Section
8
pulls the continuation sheaves back to parameter space for any given parametrized dynamical system.
For a topological space
\mathrm{\Lambda}
we define
a
parametrized dynamical system
on
X
as a continuous map

{\bm{\phi}}\colon{\mathbb{T}}\times X\times\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow X
such that

\phi^{\lambda}:={\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot,\lambda)
is dynamical system on
X
for all
\lambda\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
.
The map

\lambda{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}}}\phi^{\lambda}
, called the
transpose
, is a continuous map without additional assumptions on the topological spaces
\mathrm{\Lambda}
and
X
.
In
Section
8.2
we show that the continuation of attractors is conjugacy invariant.

Theorem
(Conjugacy Invariance Theorem, cf. Thm.
8.1
)

Let
X
,
Y
be
compact metric spaces. Suppose
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
and
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},Y)
are conjugate parametrized dynamical systems.
Then, the étalé spaces
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
and
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
are homeomorphic.

Section
9
applies the continuation sheaves to bifurcations. A stable parametrization is shown to produce locally constant sheaves.
The pull-back for the attractor sheaf is denoted by
{\mathcal{A}}^{{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}}
. In terms of sheaf cohomology with respect to
{\mathcal{A}}^{{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}}
we obtain the following result:

Theorem
(cf. Thm.
9.2
)

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}
be both contractible and locally contractible, and let

\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
be a deformation retract of
\mathrm{\Lambda}

with
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
stable on
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
.
Suppose that

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\neq 0,\quad\text{ for some }\quad k\geq 0.

Then, there exist a bifurcation point in
\lambda_{0}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}\smallsetminus\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
.

Section
10
computes attractor sheaf cohomology for the pitchfork, saddle-node, transcritical, and S-shaped bifurcations.

Theorem
(cf. Thm.
10.1
)

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
be a parametrized dynamical system over
{\mathbb{R}}
with a pitchfork bifurcation at
\lambda_{0}
. Then,

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}~\text{~ is acyclic and ~}~H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}.

Moreover,
there exists a value
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

H^{k}\bigl(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a>\lambda_{0};\\
0&\textnormal{if $k\neq 1$ or $a\leq\lambda_{0}$,}\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty)
,
Furthermore, for
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}:=(-\infty,a]
, then

H^{k}\bigl(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong 0
for all
k
and for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Different types of bifurcations can have different cohomology in their attractor sheaves, but if two systems experience the same type of bifurcation, the cohomology is isomorphic. We propose this as a tool for classifying bifurcations, in much the same way singular homology classifies topological spaces. We believe this invariant to be computable by utilizing the existing theory for combinatorial dynamics
KMV-1b
;
database
and cellular sheaf cohomology
curry2015discrete
. This will be the subject of future work.

2
Categories of dynamical systems

Throughout this paper we use the following
definition of dynamical system.
We give spaces of
dynamical systems a categorical as well as a topological structure as outlined below.

Definition 1

Let
(X,{T})
be a topological space and let
{\mathbb{T}}
be the (additive) topological monoid (or group) with topology
{T}_{\mathbb{T}}
.
A
dynamical system
is a continuous map

\phi\colon{\mathbb{T}}\times X\rightarrow X
that satisfies

(i)

\phi(0,x)=x
for all
x\in X
;

(ii)

\phi(t,\phi(s,x))=\phi(t+s,x)
for all
s,t\in{\mathbb{T}}
and all
x\in X
.

The set of all dynamical systems on the phase space
X
with time space
{\mathbb{T}}

is denoted by
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
. Also,
\phi(t,x)
may be denoted
\phi_{t}(x).

The time space
{\mathbb{T}}
is either
{\mathbb{Z}}
,
{\mathbb{Z}}^{+}
,
{\mathbb{R}}
, or
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
.
In applications, for example those arising from differential equations, it is common for the topology on
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}
(or
{\mathbb{R}}^{+}
) to be the standard topology, which we assume throughout the rest of this paper, but certain results do not require the topology
{T}_{\mathbb{T}}
to have specific properties. Certain properties of the phase space topology
{T}
do play a crucial role. In particular, for clarity of the presentation of the main ideas of this paper,
we always consider the phase space
X
to be a compact topological space
. For some results, such as Theorem
8.1
, we additionally assume a metric on
X
. Such restrictions are explicitly stated and explained when needed.

We endow
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
with a suitable topology. One natural choice arises by viewing
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
as a function space with the compact-open topology, i.e. the topology generated by the subbasis of sets of the form

\bigl\{\phi~|~\phi(K)\subset U\hbox{ for $K$ compact in ${\mathbb{T}}\times X$ and $U$ open in $X$}\bigr\}

by varying pairs
(K,U)
.

Next we endow
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
with a categorical structure and refer to
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
as the
category of dynamical systems on
X
over
{\mathbb{T}}
. An object
\phi\in{\mathsf{ob}}({\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X))
is a dynamical system
\phi\colon{\mathbb{T}}\times X\rightarrow X
. A morphism in
{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)
is defined as
\tau\times h
such that

(i)

h\colon X\rightarrow X
is a continuous map;

(ii)

\tau\colon{\mathbb{T}}\times X\rightarrow{\mathbb{T}}
is a continuous reparametrization
that is strictly monotone and bijective for each
x
and satisfies
\tau(0,x)=0
;

(iii)

the following diagram commutes. cf.
deVries
,
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}}\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbb{T}}\times X}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces X}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\tau\times h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\phi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\psi}

We refer to such a morphism
\tau\times h
as a (topological) quasiconjugacy. Note that
\hom(\phi,\psi)
can also be endowed with the compact-open topology, so that both the objects and the hom-set of
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
are topological spaces.
We abuse notation so that an open subset
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset{\mathsf{ob}}({\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X))
is referred to as an open set
\mathrm{\Omega}
in
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
.

Remark 1

Note that the conditions on reparametrizations imply that
\tau=\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits
in the case that
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{Z}}
, cf.
(
Vries2
, II(7.2))
. This in part motivates the terminology of quasiconjugacy. When
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}
, the case
h=\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits
yields a reparametrization of time
\tau(t,x)
.

Remark 2

For special subsets of dynamical systems, such as smooth flows on a manifold, topologies other than the compact-open topology
may be more appropriate.

For clarity of presentation, we use the notation
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
to mean that the objects and morphisms of this category are given the compact-open topology.

However, in other cases, similar results to those obtained for
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
follow from the abstract theory presented in Section
4
.

Remark 3

More restrictive choices of the set of morphisms lead to subcategories. For example, one may consider from least restrictive to most restrictive: topological (semi)equivalence with reparametrization of time, topological (semi)conjugacy, or no structure on the morphism set, ie.
{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\phi)=\{{\rm id}\times{\rm id}\}
and
{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)=\emptyset
when
\phi\neq\psi
, cf.
deVries
;
Robinson
.

3
Functoriality of dynamics

The study of a dynamical system often focuses on the properties of its invariant sets. A subset
S\subset X
is
invariant
if it is the union of complete orbits, or equivalently
\phi_{t}(S)=S
for all
t\in{\mathbb{T}}
. One of the most important classes of invariant sets are the attractors. In
KMV-1a
, it is shown that the set of attractors has the algebraic structure of a bounded, distributive lattice.
In this section, we characterize such algebraic structures in terms of functors on the category of dynamical systems.

For a given dynamical system
\phi\colon{\mathbb{T}}\times X\rightarrow X
and a subset
U\subset X
, the
maximal invariant set in
U
is

\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(U):=\bigcup\{S\subset U:\phi(t,U)=U\textnormal{ for all }t\in{\mathbb{T}}^{+}\}.

The
omega-limit set
of
U
is defined by

\omega_{\phi}(U):=\bigcap_{t\geq 0}{\rm cl}\,\bigcup_{s\geq t}\phi_{s}(U).

Recall from
KMV-1a
some properties of
\omega_{\phi}(U)
.

(i)

\omega_{\phi}(U)
is compact, closed, and nonempty whenever
U\neq\emptyset
,

(ii)

\omega_{\phi}(U)
is an invariant set,
1
1

1

For a forward invariant set
U
, i.e.
\phi_{t}(U)\subset U
for all
t\geq 0
, it holds that
\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}({\rm cl}\,U)=\omega_{\phi}(U)
.

(iii)

\omega_{\phi}\bigl(\omega_{\phi}(U)\bigr)=\omega_{\phi}(U)
,

(iv)

\omega_{\phi}({\rm cl}\,U)=\omega_{\phi}(U)
,

(v)

\omega_{\phi}(U\cup V)=\omega_{\phi}(U)\cup\omega_{\phi}(V)
.

A subset
U\subset X
is called an
attracting neighborhood
if
\omega_{\phi}(U)\subset{\rm int\,}U
. Attracting neighborhoods form a bounded, distributive lattice denoted by
{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
. The binary operations are
\cap
and
\cup
, see
KMV-1a
.
A subset
A\subset X
is called an
attractor
if there exists an attracting neighborhood
U\subset X
such that
A=\omega_{\phi}(U)
, which is a neighborhood of
A
by definition. Attractors are compact, closed invariant sets, and the set of all attractors is a bounded, distributive lattice
{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
with binary operations:
A\vee A^{\prime}=A\cup A^{\prime}
and

A\wedge A^{\prime}:=\omega_{\phi}(A\cap A^{\prime})
, cf.
KMV-1a
.

Remark 4

In the above listed properties of omega-limit sets and attractors, the compactness of
X
is crucial. If we drop the compactness assumption on
X
, some of the properties, such as invariance and idempotency, do not hold in general.

When the spaces
{\mathbb{T}},X
are fixed, we often write
{\mathbf{DS}}
in place of
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X).

The categorical structure of
{\mathbf{DS}}
can now be used to reformulate the above lattices in terms of functors.
For notational convenience we write
\psi_{t}^{\dagger}:=\psi(\tau(t,\cdot),\cdot)

Lemma 1

Let
\phi,\psi\in{\mathsf{ob}}({\mathbf{DS}})

and let
\tau\times h\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)
. Then, for all
U\subset Y
we have

\phi_{t}(h^{-1}(U))\subset h^{-1}(\psi^{\dagger}_{t}(U))\quad\forall t\geq 0.

In particular,

\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(U))=\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(U)))\subset h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(U)).

(2)

Proof

See Appendix
B
.

Now suppose we have
\tau\times h\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)
and
U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\psi)
. Then, by Lemma
1
,

\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(U))\subset h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(U))\subset h^{-1}(\hbox{\rm int}\,(U))\subset\hbox{\rm int}\,(h^{-1}(U)),

where the latter inclusion follows from the continuity of
f
.
Therefore
h^{-1}(U)\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi),
and the inverse image operator induces a well-defined map
h^{-1}\colon{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\psi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
.
This map is in fact a homomorphism by the properties of inverse images, since the lattice operations on
{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
and
{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\psi)
are union and intersection, so using functor notaton,
{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\tau\times h)=h^{-1}
. Thus, by assigning to each dynamical system its attracting neighborhood lattice and to each morphism its inverse image operator, by the properties of inverse images and Lemma
1
, we have a contravariant functor,
{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
, from the category of dynamical systems to the category of bounded, distributive lattices.

Remark 5

A neighborhood
U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
is an attracting block if

\phi_{t}({\rm cl}\,U)\subset{\rm int\,}(U)

for all
t>0
.
Now suppose
\tau\times h\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)
and
U\in{\mathsf{ABlock}}(\psi)
. Then for all
t>0

\displaystyle\phi_{t}\bigl({\rm cl}\,(h^{-1}(U))\bigr)

\displaystyle\subset\phi_{t}\bigl(h^{-1}({\rm cl}\,U)\bigr)\subset h^{-1}\bigl(\psi^{\dagger}_{t}({\rm cl}\,U)\bigr)

\displaystyle\subset h^{-1}(\hbox{\rm int}\,(U))\subset\hbox{\rm int}\,(h^{-1}(U)),

which implies that
h^{-1}(U)\in{\mathsf{ABlock}}(\phi)
so that we can restrict
h^{-1}
to
h^{-1}\colon{\mathsf{ABlock}}(\psi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{ABlock}}(\phi)
. As before,
{\mathsf{ABlock}}(\tau\times h)=h^{-1}
. This makes
{\mathsf{ABlock}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
a contravariant functor. We will primarily use attracting neighborhoods, but Remark
12
demonstrates that restricting to attracting blocks does not change the theory.

A similar construction can be used to define a contravariant functor
{\mathsf{Att}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
, but its action on morphisms must be modified, since the inverse image of an attractor need not be an attractor.

Proposition 1

Suppose
\tau\times h\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)
and
A\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi)
. Then
\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A))\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
. Moreover, for
\tau\times h\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi),
the map
\omega_{\phi}\circ h^{-1}\colon{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
is a lattice homomorphism.

Proof

See Appendix
B
.

Thus, by assigning each dynamical system its attractor lattice and each morphism
\tau\times h\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)
the operator
{\mathsf{Att}}(\tau\times h)=\omega_{\phi}\circ h^{-1}
, we have a contravariant functor
{\mathsf{Att}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
.

Remark 6

If
\tau\times h\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)
is a conjugacy, i.e.
h\colon X\rightarrow Y
is a homeomorphism, then
also
\tau^{-1}\times h^{-1}\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\psi,\phi)
is a conjugacy, where
\tau^{-1}(s,y)
is defined by

s=\tau(t,h^{-1}(y))
. As a consequence,
A\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
if and only if
h(A)\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi)
, cf. Appendix
B
.

Remark 7

Similar statements as in Lemma
1
also hold for
\alpha
-limit sets, as defined in
KMV-0
. Therefore we can define
functors
{\mathsf{RNbhd}},{\mathsf{RBlock}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
for repelling neighborhoods and repelling blocks analogously.
As for
{\mathsf{Att}}
, one builds
{\mathsf{Rep}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
by replacing
\omega
with
\alpha
. The details for these constructions are in Appendix
C
.

Remark 8

In some situations it is useful to consider attracting neighborhoods in the algebra of regular closed sets using analogous constructions, cf.
KMV-1b
.

Remark 9

In the spirit of Montgomery we one can also consider the semilattice of isolating neighborhoods
{\mathsf{INbhd}}(\phi)
defined by the property
\phi_{t}({\rm cl}\,U)\subset{\rm int\,}U
, cf.
Mont
. An isolated invariant set is obtained as the maximal invariant set of an isolating neighborhood:
S=\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(U)
. The semilattice of isolated invariant sets is denoted by
{\mathsf{Isol}}(\phi)
, with
S\wedge S^{\prime}=\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(S\cap S^{\prime})
. As for attractors
\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{INbhd}}(\phi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{Isol}}(\phi)
is a semi-lattice homomorphism and
{\mathsf{INbhd}}
and
{\mathsf{Isol}}
may be regarded as functors.

Given this functorial description of dynamical structures, we now turn to the primary focus of this paper, representing continuation of dynamical features in terms of sheaves over
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
. To keep the underlying theory flexible, and so as not to repeat theoretical arguments, we first introduce the underlying concepts and theorems abstractly, and then apply this general theory in specific contexts.

4
Abstract continuation

Recall from the introduction that a fundamental feature of Conley theory is that an isolated invariant set continues under perturbation of a dynamical system, which leads to the concept of continuation of isolated invariant sets.
In this section, we provide an abstract framework to expand the continuation property to algebraic structures
of dynamics.

4.1
{\mathbf{C}}
-structures and categories of elements

Let
\mathbf{D}
be a category such that
{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})
forms a topological space,
and let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a
concrete
category, i.e. there exists a faithful functor,
{\mathbf{C}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{Set}}
, into the category of sets.
In applications
\mathbf{D}
is a category of dynamical systems equipped with a topology on
{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})
,
such as
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X),
and
{\mathbf{C}}
is the category characterizing the algebraic structure of the dynamical feature to be continued, for example bounded, distributive lattices.

A
{\mathbf{C}}
-valued contravariant functor on
\mathbf{D}
is referred to as a
{\mathbf{C}}
-structure
on
\mathbf{D}
. Let
{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{G}}\colon\mathbf{D}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
be
{\mathbf{C}}
-structures and let
{\mathsf{w}}\colon{\mathsf{E}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{G}}
be a natural transformation.
For objects
\phi\in{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})
the functors
{\mathsf{E}}
and
{\mathsf{G}}
yield objects
{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)
and
{\mathsf{G}}(\phi)
in
{\mathbf{C}}
and the component
{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}
of the natural transformation yields a morphism
{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{G}}(\phi)
.

In applications, typically the functor
{\mathsf{G}}
represents a dynamical feature such as attractors, and the functor
{\mathsf{E}}
denotes a corresponding neighborhood feature such as attracting neighborhoods or attracting blocks.

Furthermore, we assume the existence of a constant, contravariant functor
{\mathsf{F}}\colon\mathbf{D}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
,
\phi{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{{\mathsf{F}}}}{\mathsf{F}}_{0}
, referred to as the
universe functor
, for which there exists an injective natural transformation

\bm{\iota}\colon{\mathsf{E}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{F}}
. In dynamics applications, when
\mathbf{D}={\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
, the universe functor assigns to each
\phi
a fixed subalgebra of the Boolean algebra
{\mathsf{Set}}(X)
, the power set of the phase space, or a fixed subalgebra of the Boolean algebra
{R}(X),
the regular closed subsets of
X
. For example, if
{\mathsf{E}}={\mathsf{ANbhd}}
, the lattice of attracting neighborhoods,
{\mathsf{E}}(\phi),
is a sublattice of
{\mathsf{Set}}(X)
.

Now we have a span of functors and natural transformations
{\mathsf{F}}\xLeftarrow{\bm{\iota}}{\mathsf{E}}\xRightarrow{{\mathsf{w}}}{\mathsf{G}}
which are summarized in the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}}}
{\mathsf{E}}
{\mathsf{F}}
\bm{\iota}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{C}}}
{\mathsf{E}}
{\mathsf{G}}
{\mathsf{w}}

(3)

Since
{\mathbf{C}}
is a concrete category, we may regard a functor
{\mathsf{E}}
into
{\mathbf{C}}
as a
{\mathbf{Set}}
-valued functor, and thus consider
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
, its
category of elements
, cf.
MacLane
;
MacLaneMoerdijk
.
The category of elements construction is used in the next section to generate an étalé space. To define the category of elements
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
, let
{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}])
be the set of all pairs
(\phi,U)
such that
\phi\in{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})
and
U\in{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)
.
The morphisms of
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
are maps
(\phi,U)\rightarrow(\phi^{\prime},U^{\prime})
for which
there is a
\mathbf{D}
-morphism
h\colon\phi\rightarrow\phi^{\prime}
with

{\mathsf{E}}(h)(U^{\prime})=U
.
The projection
(\phi,U)\mapstochar\rightarrow\phi
defines a canonical projection functor

\bm{\pi}\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]\rightarrow\mathbf{D}.

Moreover, given a natural transformation between functors,
{\mathsf{w}}\colon{\mathsf{E}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{G}},
we have the functor between the associated categories of elements

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}]\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]

\displaystyle\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]

\displaystyle(\phi,U)

\displaystyle\mapstochar\rightarrow(\phi,{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U)).

From the span of functors
{\mathsf{F}}\xLeftarrow{\bm{\iota}}{\mathsf{E}}\xRightarrow{{\mathsf{w}}}{\mathsf{G}}
we obtain a span of functors on the associated categories of elements:

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{F}}]

\displaystyle\xleftarrow{\mathrm{\Pi}[\bm{\iota}]}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]\xrightarrow{\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}]}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]

(4)

\displaystyle(\phi,U)

\displaystyle{\displaystyle\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\phantom{000\,}}}(\phi,U){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{\phantom{000\,}}}(\phi,{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U)).

Note that in (
4
), the set
U\in{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)
. To localize
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
, for a fixed element
U\in{\mathsf{F}}_{0}

we define the subcategory

\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
via

{\mathsf{ob}}\bigl(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\bigr):=\Bigl\{(\phi,U)\in{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}])~|~U\in{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)\Bigr\}

with morphisms
(\phi,U)\rightarrow(\phi^{\prime},U)
for which
there is a
\mathbf{D}
-morphism
h\colon\phi\rightarrow\phi^{\prime}
with
{\mathsf{E}}(h)(U)=U
.

Applying the projection functor
\bm{\pi}
yields a corresponding subcategory

\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
of
\mathbf{D}
. The objects of
\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
are given by

{\mathsf{ob}}\bigl(\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\bigr)=\bigl\{\phi\in{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})~|~U\in{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)\bigr\}

with morphisms
h\colon\phi\rightarrow\phi^{\prime}
with
{\mathsf{E}}(h)(U)=U
.
This yields the following commutative diagrams:

\hbox to135.73pt{\vbox to70.64pt{\pgfpicture\makeatletter\hbox{\hskip 67.86247pt\lower-35.3195pt\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} \lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke} \lxSVG@begingroup@{fill} \lxSVG@setlinewidth{\the\pgflinewidth}\lxSVG@begingroup@{stroke-width} \lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\nullfont\hbox to0.0pt{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {}{}{}{{}}\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\hbox{\hbox{{\lxSVG@begingroup@{_scopebegin} {{}{}{{{}}{{}}{{}}{{}}}{{{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces}}}{{}{}{{

(5)

where
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\phi):=\bigl(\phi,{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U)\bigr)
is called the
partial section functor
, which satisfies:

\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\circ\bm{\pi}=\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}]\hbox{\; and\; }\bm{\pi}\circ\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]={\mathsf{id}}.

(6)

We leave it to the reader to verify functoriality.

Remark 10

In settings where
\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
is a used as a subset of
\mathbf{D}
we abuse notation and write
\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
. The same applies to open subsets
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset\mathbf{D}
, cf. Sect.
2
.

4.2
Continuation frames and étalé spaces

In the same way that
{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})
forms topological space, we will equip
{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}])
with a topology. This is done such that the functors
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}},U]
become continuous maps on objects. We will abuse notation and drop the
{\mathsf{ob}}(-)
when referring to ”elements of
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
.”
A
{\mathbf{C}}
-structure
{\mathsf{E}}\colon\mathbf{D}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
is called
stable
if
\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
is open in
\mathbf{D}
for all elements
U\in{\mathsf{F}}_{0}
.
Otherwise a
{\mathbf{C}}
-structure is said to be
unstable
.
In the remainder of the paper we will always assume that
{\mathsf{E}}\colon\mathbf{D}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
admits a universe
{\mathsf{F}}_{0}
for which it is stable.

Definition 2

A
{\mathbf{C}}
-continuation frame on
\mathbf{D}
is a triple
({\mathsf{G}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{w}})

consisting of

{\mathbf{C}}
-structures
{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{G}}\colon\mathbf{D}\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
and a natural transformation

{\mathsf{w}}\colon{\mathsf{E}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{G}}

such that

(i)

{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathsf{G}}(\phi)
is surjective for all
\phi\in{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})
;

(ii)

{\mathsf{E}}
is a stable
{\mathbf{C}}
-structure.

(iii)

The sets
\bigl\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\cap\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U^{\prime}]:{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U)={\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U^{\prime})\bigr\}
are open for all pairs
U,U^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{F}}_{0}
.

Condition (i) can be paraphrased by saying that
{\mathsf{w}}
is
componentwise
surjective.
The
{\mathbf{C}}
-structure
{\mathsf{E}}
in a continuation frame is
is called a
stable extension
of
{\mathsf{G}}
.

Condition (iii) is crucial
for continuity of the sections
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]
.
In the application of
{\mathbf{C}}
-structures in dynamics,

{\mathsf{G}}
is typically unstable as the examples in the next section show.

The next step is to
topologize
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
with the topology generated by

{B}({\mathsf{G}}):=\Bigl\{\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega})~|~U\in{\mathsf{F}}_{0},\,\hbox{$\mathrm{\Omega}\subset\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]$ open}\Bigr\},

where
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega})
is the image under
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]
of objects
\phi\in\mathrm{\Omega}
.

Lemma 2

{B}({\mathsf{G}})
is a basis for a
topology on
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
. The maps
\mathrm{\Theta[{\mathsf{w}};U]}\colon\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
are all continuous.

Proof

Let
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega_{1}})
,
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega_{2}})
be some elements of
{B}({\mathsf{G}})
. We can write their intersection in the following way:

\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega_{1}})\cap\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega_{2}})=\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega}),

where we let
\mathrm{\Omega}=\mathrm{\Omega}_{1}\cap\mathrm{\Omega}_{2}\cap\bigl\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\cap\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U^{\prime}]:{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U)={\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U^{\prime})\bigr\}
. For any given
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]
and basis element
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega})
, one has

\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]^{-1}\big(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega})\big)=\mathrm{\Omega}\cap\bigl\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\cap\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U^{\prime}]:{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U)={\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U^{\prime})\bigr\},

which is open.

The functor
\bm{\pi}\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]\rightarrow\mathbf{D}
may be regarded as a projection

\pi\colon{\mathsf{ob}}\bigl(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]\bigr)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D}),

and with the above defined topology on
{\mathsf{ob}}\bigl(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]\bigr)
, it is also a continuous map between topological spaces.
In this setting we denote the objects of the category of elements by
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
,
and we show that
(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}],\pi)
is an
étalé space
in the category
{\mathbf{Set}}
by establishing that
\pi
is a local homeomorphism.

Theorem 4.1

Let
({\mathsf{G}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{w}})
be a
{\mathbf{C}}
-continuation frame on
\mathbf{D}
. Then, the pair
(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}],\pi)
is an étalé space on
\mathbf{D}
.

Proof

To establish
(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}],\pi)
as an étalé space with the above defined topology on
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
we show that
\pi
is a local homeomorphism.

Let
(\phi,S)
be a point in
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
. Then, since
{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathsf{G}}(\phi)
is surjective for all
\phi
, there exists
U\in{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)
such that
{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U)=S
. Consequently, the point
(\phi,S)
is contained in
the image of the map

\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\colon\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
,
which is
open
by the definition of the topology. The image under
\pi
of the set

\textnormal{Im}\bigl(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\bigr)

is the set
\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
which is open by assumption. It remains to show that

\pi\colon\textnormal{Im}\big(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\big)\rightarrow\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]

is a homeomorphism.

First we show bijectivity.
By definition

\pi\colon\textnormal{Im}\big(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\big)\rightarrow\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]

is onto and since
\phi\mapstochar\rightarrow(\phi,{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U))
for
\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
is a section by Lemma
2
,
we establish bijectivity.

Second we show that

\pi\colon\textnormal{Im}\big(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\big)\rightarrow\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]

is continuous and open.
Let
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
be open. Then,

\pi^{-1}(\mathrm{\Omega})=\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega})

is open by the definition of the topology which proves the continuity of
\pi
.
Let

\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega})
be a basic open set.
Then,

\pi\big(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega})\cap\textnormal{Im}\bigl(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\bigr)\big)=\mathrm{\Omega}\cap\bigl\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\cap\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U^{\prime}]:{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U)={\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U^{\prime})\bigr\}

is open, and thus
\pi
is a homeomorphism. This proves that
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
is an étalé space in
{\mathbf{Set}}
.

In the spirit of
Mont
two points
(\phi,S)
and
(\phi^{\prime},S^{\prime})
are
related by continuation
if they are contained in the same quasicomponent of
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
, or equivalently
(\phi^{\prime},S^{\prime})
is contained in the quasicomponent of
(\phi,S)
. Recall that a quasicomponent of
(\phi,S)
of
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
is the intersection of all clopen subsets of
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
containing
(\phi,S)
.
The following result characterizes this topology.

Proposition 2

Let
({\mathsf{G}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{w}})
be a
{\mathbf{C}}
-continuation frame. The topology generated by the basis
{B}({\mathsf{G}})
is the coarsest topology such that the maps
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\colon\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi[{\mathsf{G}}]}
are continuous, and
\pi\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]\rightarrow\mathbf{D}
is a local homeomorphism.

Proof

Theorem
4.1
and Lemma
2
give us that
{B}({\mathsf{G}})
generates such a topology. Now suppose the maps
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\colon\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi[{\mathsf{G}}]}
are continuous, and
\pi\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]\rightarrow\mathbf{D}
is a local homeomorphism in some topology
\tau
. Let
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
be an open set in
\mathbf{D}
for some
U
. We have that
\pi\circ\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]=\iota
where
\iota
denotes the inclusion of
\mathrm{\Omega}
into
\mathbf{D}
. Since
\iota
and
\pi
are both local homeomorphisms, so is
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]
, which implies that the image
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega})
is in
\tau
. Every element of
{B}({\mathsf{G}})
is of this form; thus,
{B}({\mathsf{G}})
is coarser than
\tau
.

The stable
{\mathbf{C}}
-structure in a
{\mathbf{C}}
-continuation frame yields a second étalé space.
Topologize
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
as follows.
Define the embedding
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{id}};U]\colon\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
as the trivial section
\phi\mapstochar\rightarrow(\phi,U)
and define a subbasis for the topology on
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
as follows:

{B}({\mathsf{E}}):=\Bigl\{\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{id}};U](\mathrm{\Omega})~|~U\in{\mathsf{F}}_{0},\,\hbox{$\mathrm{\Omega}\subset\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]$ open}\Bigr\}.

Corollary 1

Let
({\mathsf{G}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{w}})
be a
{\mathbf{C}}
-continuation frame on
\mathbf{D}
. Then, the pair
(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}],\pi)
is an étalé space on
\mathbf{D}
.

Proof

The projection
\pi\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]\rightarrow{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})
given by
(\phi,U)\mapstochar\rightarrow\phi
is a local homeomorphism with the above defined topology, i.e.

\pi\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
is a homeomorphism.

The category of elements
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{F}}]
trivially gives an étalé space and makes the span of functors in (
4
) into a span of étalé spaces. A continuous map
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}]\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
is called an
étalé morphism
if the following diagram in commutes, cf.
(
Tenni
, Definition 3.3)
.

\begin{diagram}

(7)

Such a map is then a local homeomorphism by

(
Borceux
, Proposition 2.4.8)
,
(
Tenni
, Lemma 3.5)
.

Corollary 2

The map
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}]\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
is an étalé morphism.

Proof

By definition of the topologies on
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
and
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
, the inverse image under
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}]
of a basis element
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega})
,
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset\mathbf{D}
open, is open in
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
, which proves that
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}]
is continuous.

4.3
Induced étalé morphisms

The following lemma provides a criterion to construct an étalé space morphism from a natural transformation of structures.

Lemma 3

Let
{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}
be stable
{\mathbf{C}}
-structures on
\mathbf{D}
and let
{\mathsf{n}}\colon{\mathsf{E}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}
be a natural transformation. Then, the induced functor
\mathrm{\Pi}[{{\mathsf{n}}}]\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}]
, defined by

(\phi,U)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\phi,{{\mathsf{n}}}_{\phi}(U)),

defines a morphism of étalé spaces if and only if the sets

\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]:{{\mathsf{n}}}_{\phi}(U)=U^{\prime}\},

are open for every pair
U,U^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{F}}_{0}
.

Proof

Suppose
\mathrm{\Pi}[{{\mathsf{n}}}]
is continuous, and
U,U^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{F}}_{0}
. Then,

\big\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]~|~{{\mathsf{n}}}_{\phi}(U)=U^{\prime}\big\}=\pi\Big(\mathrm{\Pi}[{{\mathsf{n}}}]^{-1}\big(\textnormal{Im}(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{id}};U])\big)\cap\textnormal{Im}(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{id}};U^{\prime}])\Big),

which is open. Now for the converse. Let
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{id}};U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega})
be a subbasis element of
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}]
. Then,

\bigcup_{U\in{\mathsf{F}}}\Big(\mathrm{\Omega}\cap\big\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]~|~{{\mathsf{n}}}_{\phi}(U)=U^{\prime}\big\}\Big)=\mathrm{\Pi}[{{\mathsf{n}}}]^{-1}\big(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{id}};U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega})\big),

which is a union of open sets, and therefore open.

When the action of
{{\mathsf{n}}}_{\phi}
is independent of
\phi\in{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})
, the openness condition is trivially satisfied. This condition for stable structures translates to unstable structures in the following proposition.

Proposition 3

Let
({\mathsf{G}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{w}})
and
({\mathsf{G}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime})
be continuation frames on
\mathbf{D}
, and
let
{\mathsf{v}}\colon{\mathsf{G}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}
be a natural transformation. Suppose there exists a natural transformation
\widetilde{{\mathsf{v}}}\colon{\mathsf{E}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}
such that
\mathrm{\Pi}[\widetilde{{\mathsf{v}}}]
is continuous, and the following diagram commutes:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{E}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{G}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\widetilde{{\mathsf{v}}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{w}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{v}}}

(8)

Then,
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{v}}]
is a morphism of étalé spaces. The lift
\widetilde{{\mathsf{v}}}
is called a stable extension of
{\mathsf{v}}
.

Proof

We have the following maps on étalé spaces:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathbf{D}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}{[}\widetilde{{\mathsf{v}}}{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}{[}{\mathsf{w}}{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}{[}{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime}{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}{[}{\mathsf{v}}{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}

By Corollary
2

\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}]
and
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime}]
are étalé morphisms and by
(
Borceux
, Proposition 2.4.8)
,
(
Tenni
, Lemma 3.5)
the map
\mathrm{\Pi}[\tilde{\mathsf{v}}]
is an étalé morphism. Diagram chasing then shows that
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{v}}]
is also an étalé morphism by using the same results.

5
Continuation of attractors and Morse representations

In this section we establish continuation frames for attractors and for finite sublattices of attractors. The latter induces continuation of Morse representations.

5.1
Attractors

In Section
3
we establised the functors
{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
and
{\mathsf{Att}}
acting between the category of dynamical systems and the category of bounded, distributive lattices. The topologies introduced in Section
2
yield the following result.

Lemma 4

{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
is a stable structure.

Proof

As subset of
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
we define

\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]:=\{\phi\in{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)~|~U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)\}
for any subset
U\subset X
.
The condition
U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
is equivalent to

\omega_{\phi}(U)\subset{\rm int\,}U
. By
GKV
;
KMV-1a
we have the equivalent characterization:

U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
if and only if there exists a time
\tau>0
such that

\phi_{t}({\rm cl}\,U)\subset{\rm int\,}U\quad\forall t\geq\tau,

(9)

which is equivalent to

\bigcup_{t\in[\tau,2\tau]}\phi_{t}({\rm cl}\,U)=\phi\bigl([\tau,2\tau]\times{\rm cl}\,U\bigr)\subset{\rm int\,}U.

(10)

Indeed, if (
9
) is satisfied then (
10
) follows. On the other hand if
(
10
) is satisfied then

\displaystyle\phi\bigl([2\tau,3\tau]\times{\rm cl}\,U\bigr)

\displaystyle=\bigcup_{t\in[\tau,2\tau]}\phi_{t+\tau}({\rm cl}\,U)=\phi_{\tau}\Bigl(\bigcup_{t\in[\tau,2\tau]}\phi_{t}({\rm cl}\,U)\Bigr)

\displaystyle=\phi_{\tau}({\rm int\,}U)\subset\phi_{\tau}({\rm cl}\,U)\subset{\rm int\,}U.

By induction
\phi\bigl([n\tau,(n+1)\tau]\times{\rm cl}\,U\bigr)\subset{\rm int\,}U
for all
n\geq 1
, which establishes (
9
).
Summarizing,

\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]~\text{~~~ if and only~~~}~\phi\bigl([\tau,2\tau]\times{\rm cl}\,U\bigr)\subset{\rm int\,}U\;\text{for some~}~\tau>0.

(11)

For any
\tau>0
define

K_{\tau}=[\tau,2\tau]\times{\rm cl}\,U\subset{\mathbb{T}}\times X
which is a compact set.
Consider the basic open sets

{\mathcal{B}}\bigl(K_{\tau},{\rm int\,}U\bigr):=\Bigl\{\phi~|~\phi(K_{\tau})=\phi\big([\tau,2\tau]\times{\rm cl}\,U\big)\subset{\rm int\,}U\Bigr\},

which are contained in the subbasis for the compact-open topology on
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
. By (
11
) an element
\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]

is contained in
{\mathcal{B}}\bigl(K_{\tau},{\rm int\,}U\bigr)
for some
\tau>0

and thus
\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]\subset\bigcup_{\tau>0}{\mathcal{B}}\bigl(K_{\tau},{\rm int\,}U\bigr)
.
On the other hand if
\phi\in{\mathcal{B}}\bigl(K_{\tau},{\rm int\,}U\bigr)
for some
\tau>0
, then (
11
)
implies that
\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]
which shows that
\bigcup_{\tau>0}{\mathcal{B}}\bigl(K_{\tau},{\rm int\,}U\bigr)\subset\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]
and thus
\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]=\bigcup_{\tau>0}{\mathcal{B}}\bigl(K_{\tau},{\rm int\,}U\bigr)

which is a union of basic open set and thus open.

The next result we prove for isolating neighborhoods, cf. Remark
9
,
which applies to the special case of attracting neighborhoods.

Lemma 5

The sets
\bigl\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{INbhd}};U]\cap\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{INbhd}};U^{\prime}]:\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(U)=\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(U^{\prime})\bigr\}
are open in
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
.

Proof

The proof is identical to Montgomery’s proof in
Mont
for flows. Let
U_{1},U_{2}\in{\mathsf{INbhd}}(\phi)
for
\phi\in{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
. If
\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(U_{1})=\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(U_{2})=S
, then
\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(U)=S
for
U:=U_{1}\cap U_{2}
. For any point
x\in V_{i}:={\rm cl}\,U_{i}\smallsetminus\hbox{\rm int}\,U,i=1,2
, there exists a time
\tau>0
such that
\phi(\tau,x)\in X\smallsetminus V_{i}
. By compactness, we may in fact pick
\tau>0
such that
\phi(\tau,V_{i})\subset X\smallsetminus V_{i}
. The set

\displaystyle\mathrm{\Omega}=\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{INbhd}};U_{1}]\cap\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{INbhd}};U_{2}]

\displaystyle\cap\{\phi\in{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X):\phi(\tau,V_{1})\subset X\smallsetminus V_{1}\}

\displaystyle\cap\{\phi\in{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X):\phi(\tau,V_{2})\subset X\smallsetminus V_{2}\},

is open in the compact-open topology. For any
\psi\in\mathrm{\Omega}
,
\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\psi}(U_{1})=\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\psi}(U)=\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\psi}(U_{2})
, so we are done.

Remark 11

Similar to attractors, the triple
({\mathsf{INbhd}},{\mathsf{Isol}},\mbox{\rm Inv})
is a
{\mathbf{MLat}}
-continuation frame on
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
, cf.
Mont
.

In particular,
\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(U)=\omega_{\phi}(U)
when
U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
by Corollary 3.6 of
KMV-1a
. Consequently, the triple
\big({\mathsf{Att}},{\mathsf{ANbhd}},\omega\big)
is a
{\mathbf{BDLat}}
-continuation frame on
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)

and
{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
is a stable extension for
{\mathsf{Att}}
.
By
Theorem
4.1
we have that
(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}],\pi)
is an étalé space in
{\mathbf{Set}}
.

Remark 12

Stable extensions in a continuation frame are not unique. Following Remark
5
, attracting blocks define attractors via

\omega_{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{ABlock}}(\phi)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
. As before we may regard
{\mathsf{ABlock}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
as a contravariant functor which is a stable extension of
{\mathsf{Att}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
.
Using the inclusion transformation
\iota\colon{\mathsf{ABlock}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
,
we obtain the following commutative diagram of transformations:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{ABlock}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{ANbhd}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Att}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Att}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\iota}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\omega}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits}

Proposition
3
obtains an isomorphism between the étalé spaces generated from the two continuation frames
({\mathsf{Att}},{\mathsf{ANbhd}},\mathrm{\omega})
and
({\mathsf{Att}},{\mathsf{ABlock}},\mathrm{\omega})
.

The functor
{\mathsf{Att}}
is the structure we wish to continue, with stable extension
\omega\colon{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{Att}}
. This gives

We have the partial section map

\mathrm{\Theta}[\omega;U]\colon\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}],

which maps a dynamical system
\phi
with attracting neighborhood
U
to the pair
(\phi,A)
with its associated attractor
A=\omega_{\phi}(U)
. Since
\omega_{\phi}
is surjective, given a pair
(\phi,A)\in\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
, there exists an attracting neighborhood
U
such that
\mathrm{\Theta}[\omega;U](\phi)=(\phi,A)
.

Remark 13

Following Remark
7
, we can build a continuation frame
({\mathsf{Rep}},{\mathsf{RNbhd}},\alpha)
for repellers, which gives us an étalé space
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Rep}}]
. Proposition
3
and the setup in Appendix
C
allow us to construct an isomorphism of étalé spaces from the dual repeller operator

\mathrm{\Pi}[*]\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Rep}}]\quad(\phi,A)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\phi,A^{*}),

by using the set complement on attracting neighborhoods as a stable extension of
*
. To view set complement and
*
as natural transformations, one can augment the hom-set of
{\mathbf{BDLat}}
with anti-homomorphisms or compose with an opposite functor. This technicality appears again in
5.2
with
\bm{\mu}
and
\bm{\tau}
. Alas, note that so far these are
{\mathbf{Set}}
-valued étalé spaces. When we introduce lattice operations in Section
6
, this will become an anti-isomorphism of
{\mathbf{BDLat}}
-valued étalé spaces.

Remark 14

Note that
the dual repeller operator
*
is dependent on the underlying system
\phi
:

A\mapstochar\rightarrow A^{*}=\{x\in X:\omega_{\phi}(x)\cap A=\emptyset\}.

(12)

For convenience of notation, we will omit the subscript when the underlying system is understood.

5.2
Morse representations

Define the set
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
consisting of all the finite sublattices
{\mathsf{A}}\subset{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
. Every finite sublattice is understood to contain at least the elements
\emptyset
and
\omega_{\phi}(X)
.
The set of finite sublattices can be given the structure of a
semibounded
lattice

{\mathsf{A}}\wedge{\mathsf{A}}^{\prime}:={\mathsf{A}}\cap{\mathsf{A}}^{\prime},\quad{\mathsf{A}}\vee{\mathsf{A}}^{\prime}:=[{\mathsf{A}}\cup{\mathsf{A}}^{\prime}],\quad{\mathsf{A}},{\mathsf{A}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi),

where
[{\mathsf{A}}\cup{\mathsf{A}}^{\prime}]
is the smallest sublattice containing
{\mathsf{A}}\cup{\mathsf{A}}^{\prime}
. Note that, since the
{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
may be infinite, there may be no maximal element in
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
, and hence
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
may not be a bounded lattice.
The lattice
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
has minimal element
\{\emptyset,\omega_{\phi}(X)\}
.
Also
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
is not a distributive lattice in general. The assignment
{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)\rightarrow{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
may be regarded as covariant functor
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}\colon{\mathbf{BDLat}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{Lat}}
, where
{\mathbf{Lat}}
is the category of lattices.
Indeed, if
{\mathsf{L}}
and
{\mathsf{K}}
are bounded, distributive lattices and
g\colon{\mathsf{L}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{K}}
is a lattice homomorphism (preserves
0
and
1
), then the inclusion of a finite sublattice
i\colon{\mathsf{L}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathsf{L}}
defines a finite sublattice
{\mathsf{K}}^{\prime}\subset{\mathsf{K}}
as the range of the composition
g\circ i
. We define the arrow

{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}(g)\colon{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{L}}\rightarrow{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{K}},\quad{\mathsf{L}}^{\prime}\mapstochar\rightarrow{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}(g)({\mathsf{L}}^{\prime}):={\mathsf{K}}^{\prime}.

The composition of functors yields the the contravariant functors
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}\circ{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
and
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}\circ{\mathsf{Att}}
which provide the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Lat}}}
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Set}}
\bm{\mathsf{\iota}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Lat}}}
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}
\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}

(13)

where
\bm{\mathsf{\iota}}
is the natural transformation defined by inclusion. The bounded lattice
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Set}}(X)
consists of finite rings of sets over
X
in the universe for the continuation frame we construct.
Moreover,
\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}
is a natural transformation defined as follows.
Let
{\mathsf{U}}\in{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
, then
\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}}):=\{A=\omega_{\phi}(U)~|~U\in{\mathsf{U}}\}
.
This construction also yields the lattice homomorphism
\omega_{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{U}}\twoheadrightarrow\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}})
.
From the definition of
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
we obtain the following lemma.

Lemma 6

{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)\rightarrow{\mathbf{Lat}}
is a stable structure. The sets
\{\phi\in{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X):\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}})=\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}}^{\prime})\}
are open.

Proof

For any finite sublattice
{\mathsf{U}}\in{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Set}}(X)
we observe that

\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}};{\mathsf{U}}]=\bigcap_{U\in{\mathsf{U}}}\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U],

which, by Lemma
4
, is open. Suppose we have two finite sublattices
{\mathsf{U}},{\mathsf{U}}^{\prime}\in{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\psi)
such that
\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\psi}({\mathsf{U}})=\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\psi}({\mathsf{U}}^{\prime})
. Then for each
U\in{\mathsf{U}}
, there exists a
U^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{U}}^{\prime}
such that
\omega_{\psi}(U)=\omega_{\psi}(U^{\prime})
, and vice versa. Take a finite intersection of open sets

\bigcap_{\begin{subarray}{c}U\in{\mathsf{U}},U^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{U}}^{\prime}\\
\omega_{\psi}(U)=\omega_{\psi}(U^{\prime})\end{subarray}}\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]\cap\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U^{\prime}]:\omega_{\phi}(U)=\omega_{\phi}(U^{\prime})\}

\subset\{\phi\in\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}};{\mathsf{U}}]\cap\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}};{\mathsf{U}}^{\prime}]:\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}})=\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}}^{\prime})\},

and we are done.

By construction the natural transformation
\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}\colon{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\Longrightarrow{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}
is surjective, which in combination with Lemma
6
implies the following:

Lemma 7

The triple
\bigl({\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}},{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}},\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}\bigr)
is a
{\mathbf{Lat}}
-continuation frame.

Following
KastiKV
;
KMV-1c
we associate an ordered partition
{\mathsf{T}}({\mathsf{U}})
for every finite
sublattice
{\mathsf{U}}\in{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
: let
{\mathsf{J}}({\mathsf{U}})
be the poset of join-irreducible elements in
{\mathsf{U}}
, elements
U
with a unique predecessor
\overleftarrow{U}
, and
for
U\in{\mathsf{J}}({\mathsf{U}})
define
T(U)=U\smallsetminus\overleftarrow{U}
. The poset
{\mathsf{J}}({\mathsf{U}})
, ordered by inclusion, induces an isomorphic poset structure on
{\mathsf{T}}({\mathsf{U}}):=\{T(U)~|~U\in{\mathsf{J}}({\mathsf{U}})\}

with
T(U)\leq T(U^{\prime})
if and only if
U\subseteq U^{\prime}
.
The poset
{\mathsf{T}}({\mathsf{U}})
is called a
Morse tessellation
for
\phi
.
We can similarly consider a finite sublattice
{\mathsf{A}}\in{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
, and let
{\mathsf{J}}({\mathsf{A}})
be the poset of join-irreducible elements in
{\mathsf{A}}
.
Define a map
{\mathsf{J}}({\mathsf{A}})\rightarrow{\mathsf{Invset}}(\phi)

by
A\mapstochar\rightarrow M(A):=A\cap(\overleftarrow{A})^{*}=C_{\mathsf{Att}}(A,\overleftarrow{A})
. The elements
M(A)
compose the isomorphic poset
{\mathsf{M}}({\mathsf{A}}):=\{M(A)~|~A\in{\mathsf{J}}({\mathsf{A}})\}

with
M(A)\leq M(A^{\prime})
if and only if
A\subseteq A^{\prime}
.
The poset
{\mathsf{M}}({\mathsf{A}})
is called a
Morse representation
for
\phi
.

Let
{\mathsf{MRepr}}(\phi)
denote the set of Morse representations for a dynamical system
\phi
, and
{\mathsf{MTess}}(\phi)
the set of Morse tessellations. There are bijections:

\bm{\tau}_{\phi}\colon{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{MTess}}(\phi),\quad\bm{\tau}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}}):={\mathsf{T}}({\mathsf{U}}),~~\bm{\tau}^{-1}_{\phi}({\mathsf{T}}):=\bigl\{U=\bigcup I~|~I\in{\mathsf{O}}({\mathsf{T}})\bigr\}.

\bm{\mu}_{\phi}\colon{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{MRepr}}(\phi),\quad\bm{\mu}_{\phi}({\mathsf{A}}):={\mathsf{M}}({\mathsf{A}}),~~\bm{\mu}^{-1}_{\phi}({\mathsf{M}}):=\bigl\{A=\bigcup_{M\in I}W^{u}(M)~|~I\in{\mathsf{O}}({\mathsf{T}})\bigr\}.

These bijections let
{\mathsf{MRepr}}(\phi)
and
{\mathsf{MTess}}(\phi)
inherit the lattice structure of their dual counterparts
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
and
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
respectively:

{\mathsf{T}}\vee{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}:=\bm{\tau}_{\phi}\bigl(\bm{\tau}_{\phi}^{-1}({\mathsf{T}})\wedge\bm{\tau}_{\phi}^{-1}({\mathsf{T}}^{\prime})\bigr),\quad{\mathsf{T}}\wedge{\mathsf{T}}^{\prime}:=\bm{\tau}_{\phi}\bigl(\bm{\tau}_{\phi}^{-1}({\mathsf{T}})\vee\bm{\tau}_{\phi}^{-1}({\mathsf{T}}^{\prime})\bigr)

{\mathsf{M}}\vee{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}:=\bm{\mu}_{\phi}\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{\phi}^{-1}({\mathsf{M}})\wedge\bm{\mu}_{\phi}^{-1}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})\bigr),\quad{\mathsf{M}}\wedge{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}:=\bm{\mu}_{\phi}\bigl(\bm{\mu}_{\phi}^{-1}({\mathsf{M}})\vee\bm{\mu}_{\phi}^{-1}({\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})\bigr).

As such,
\bm{\tau}
and
\bm{\mu}
become lattice isomorphisms. We can view
{\mathsf{MRepr}}
and
{\mathsf{MTess}}
as functors assigning dynamical systems their Morse representations and Morse tesselations respectively. Define an action on morphisms using
\bm{\tau}
and
\bm{\mu}
:

h\in\hom(\phi,\psi),\quad{\mathsf{MTess}}(h):=\bm{\tau}^{-1}\circ{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(h)\circ\bm{\tau},\quad{\mathsf{MRepr}}(h):=\bm{\mu}^{-1}\circ{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}(h)\circ\bm{\mu}.

\bm{\tau}
and
\bm{\mu}
become natural transformations in this way. The result is the following diagram of functors:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Lat}}}
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
{\mathsf{MTess}}
\bm{\tau}
\bm{\tau}^{-1}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Lat}}}
{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}
{\mathsf{MRepr}}
\bm{\mu}
\bm{\mu}^{-1}

(14)

Let
\bm{\mathsf{\Delta}}
be the natural transformation defined by

\bm{\mathsf{\Delta}}:=\bm{\mu}\circ\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}\circ\bm{\tau}^{-1}.

Given a Morse tessellation
{\mathsf{T}}
we obtain a Morse representation via
{\mathsf{T}}\mapstochar\rightarrow\bm{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{T}})=:{\mathsf{M}}
.
By the above correspondences
{\mathsf{M}}=\bm{\mu}_{\phi}({\mathsf{A}})
with
{\mathsf{A}}=\bm{\mu}_{\phi}^{-1}(\bm{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{T}}))
and

{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{\tau}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}})
with
{\mathsf{U}}=\bm{\tau}^{-1}_{\phi}({\mathsf{T}})

and
\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}(\bm{\tau}^{-1}_{\phi}({\mathsf{T}}))={\mathsf{A}}
.
For the elements
{\mathsf{U}}
and
\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}})
we have the homomorphism
\omega_{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{U}}\twoheadrightarrow{\mathsf{A}}=\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}})
. This implies the following relation for Morse tessellations and Morse representations. Associated with
{\mathsf{U}}
we have
{\mathsf{T}}=\bm{\tau}_{\phi}({\mathsf{U}})
maps to
{\mathsf{M}}=\bm{\mathsf{\Delta}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{T}})
and we obtain a canonical embedding
\iota\colon{\mathsf{M}}\hookrightarrow{\mathsf{T}}
,
which will be called a
tessellated Morse decomposition
. The embedding
\iota
is induced by the homomorphism
\omega_{\phi}
.
The continuation frame is given by the following diagrams:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Lat}}}
{\mathsf{MTess}}
{\mathsf{OrdTess}}
\bm{\mathsf{\iota}}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Lat}}}
{\mathsf{MTess}}
{\mathsf{MRepr}}
\bm{\mathsf{\Delta}}

(15)

and establishes the continuation frame

\bigl({\mathsf{MRepr}},{\mathsf{MTess}},\bm{\mathsf{\Delta}}\bigr)
. Here the universe is given by
{\mathsf{OrdTess}}(X)
which is the (complete) bounded lattice
of finite ordered tessellations of
X
.
In Section
7
we define the Morse representation sheaf which generalizes
fran3
.

Remark 15

The lattice operations on
{\mathsf{MRepr}}(\phi)
and
{\mathsf{MTess}}(\phi)
are motivated by the duality between (Priestley) pre-orders and sublattices, i.e. for a bounded distributive lattice
{\mathsf{L}}
there exist an anti-isomorphism to the lattice of
Priestley pre-order on the Priestley
{\mathsf{\Sigma}}{\mathsf{L}}
and the lattice
{\rm sub}~{\mathsf{L}}
of sublattices of
{\mathsf{L}}
, cf.
(
Schmid
, Thm. 3.7)
,
(
Cignoli
, Thm. 2.5)
.

6
Algebraic constructions

In this section we incorporate the binary operations of lattices, groups, rings, etc. and augment the étalé spaces with these operations.

6.1
Binary operations and lattices

Given two étalé spaces
(\mathrm{\Pi},\pi),(\mathrm{\Pi}^{\prime},\pi^{\prime})
over a topological space. Define

\mathrm{\Pi}\bullet\mathrm{\Pi}^{\prime}:=\big\{(\sigma,\sigma^{\prime})\in\mathrm{\Pi}\times\mathrm{\Pi}^{\prime}:\pi(\sigma)=\pi^{\prime}(\sigma^{\prime})\big\},

which is also an étalé space with the same projection map and the product topology, cf.
(
Tenni
, Sect. 2.5)
.

Proposition 4

Suppose the category
{\mathbf{C}}
has concrete binary products.
Let
({\mathsf{G}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{w}}),({\mathsf{G}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime})
be
{\mathbf{C}}
-continuation frames on
\mathbf{D}
. Then,
({\mathsf{G}}\times{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{E}}\times{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime},{\mathsf{w}}\times{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime})
is a continuation frame and the map

g\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}\times{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]\bullet\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}],\qquad\big(\phi,(S,S^{\prime})\big)\mapstochar\rightarrow\bigl((\phi,S),(\phi,S^{\prime})\bigr)

is a homeomorphism.

Proof

Since both
{\mathsf{w}}
and
{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime}
are surjective componentwise, so is
{\mathsf{w}}\times{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime}
. For the openness conditions:

\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{G}}\times{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime};(U,U^{\prime})]=\big\{\phi\in{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})~|~(U,U^{\prime})\in({\mathsf{G}}\times{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime})(\phi)\big\}=\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{G}};U]\cap\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime};U^{\prime}].

\displaystyle\{\phi\in\mathbf{D}:({\mathsf{w}}\times{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime})_{\phi}(U_{1},U_{2})=({\mathsf{w}}\times{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime})_{\phi}(U_{1}^{\prime},U_{2}^{\prime})\}=

\displaystyle\{\phi\in\mathbf{D}:{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U_{1})={\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}(U_{1}^{\prime})\}

\displaystyle\cap\{\phi\in\mathbf{D}:{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime}_{\phi}(U_{2})={\mathsf{w}}^{\prime}_{\phi}(U_{2}^{\prime})\}

which is open. Bijectivity of
g
is immediate; it remains to be shown that
g
is continuous and open on subbasis elements. Let
U,U^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{F}}
and
\mathrm{\Omega},\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}
open in
\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
and
\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime};U^{\prime}]
respectively.

Then,

g^{-1}\Big(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega})\times\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime})\cap\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]\bullet\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}]\Big)=\mathrm{\Theta}\big[{\mathsf{w}}\times{\mathsf{w}};(U,U^{\prime})\big](\mathrm{\Omega}\cap\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}),

which is open. Similarly, letting
U,U^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{F}}
and
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}}\times{\mathsf{E}}^{\prime};(U,U^{\prime})]
open we have:

g\Big(\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}}\times{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime};(U,U^{\prime})]\Big)=\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U](\mathrm{\Omega})\times\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}}^{\prime},U^{\prime}](\mathrm{\Omega})\cap\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]\bullet\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}^{\prime}]

which proves that
g
is an open map and therefore a homeomorphism.

Remark 16

The universe functor in the product continuation frame is the product
{\mathsf{F}}\times{\mathsf{F}}^{\prime}
.

We can apply Propositions
3
and
4
to the
the
{\mathbf{BDLat}}
-continuation frames
({\mathsf{Att}},{\mathsf{ANbhd}},\omega)

and
({\mathsf{Rep}},{\mathsf{RNbhd}},\alpha)

to interpret lattice operations as morphisms of étalé spaces. This permits us to regard

\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
and
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Rep}}]
as

{\mathbf{BDLat}}
-valued.

For example
\wedge_{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)\times{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
given by
(A,A^{\prime})\mapstochar\rightarrow A\wedge A^{\prime}
forms a natural transformation

\wedge\colon{\mathsf{Att}}\times{\mathsf{Att}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{Att}}.

From Proposition
3

\widetilde{\wedge}\colon{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\times{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
,
given by
(U,U^{\prime})\mapstochar\rightarrow U\cap U^{\prime}
with
\omega_{\phi}(U)=A
and

\omega_{\phi}(U^{\prime})=A^{\prime}
,
acts as a lift for
\wedge

which yields
an étalé space morphism from
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}\times{\mathsf{Att}}]
to
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
.
Combining the latter with Proposition
4
yields an étalé space morphism:

\mathrm{\Pi}[\wedge]\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]\bullet\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}],\quad\bigl((\phi,A),(\phi,A^{\prime})\bigr)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\phi,A\wedge A^{\prime}),

which establishes
\wedge
as a continuous binary operation on
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
. The same can be achieved for
\vee
.
Absorption, distributivity, and associativity follows immediately from the properties of
\wedge
and
\vee
.
It remains to show that the assignments of the neutral elements

\phi\mapstochar\rightarrow(\phi,\emptyset)\in\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}],\qquad\phi\mapstochar\rightarrow\bigl(\phi,\omega_{\phi}(X)\bigr)\in\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}],

are continuous. By composing the constant sections
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{id}};\emptyset],\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{id}};X]\colon{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}}]
with the continuous map
\mathrm{\Pi}[\omega]
we obtain the desired result.
A similar argument holds for
{\mathsf{Rep}}
. Consequently, we have established
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
and
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Rep}}]
as
{\mathbf{BDLat}}
-valued étalé spaces.
We later explore abelian structures and ring structures which are used in the treatment of sheaf cohomology.

6.2
The Conley form

Recall that the Conley form assigns to two attractors
A,A^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
an associated invariant set
(A,A^{\prime})\mapstochar\rightarrow{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}(A,A^{\prime}):=A\cap A^{\prime*}
, where

A^{\prime*}\in{\mathsf{Rep}}(\phi)
is dual to
A^{\prime}
in the sense that
A^{\prime*}=\alpha_{\phi}(U^{c})
where

U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
with
\omega_{\phi}(U)=A^{\prime}
. The repeller
A^{\prime*}
is called the
dual repeller
to
A^{\prime}
.
The Conley form has a universal property in the sense that it is a unique extension
of set-difference for bounded, distributive lattices, cf.
KMV-1c
.

A
Morse neighborhood
is a subset
T\subset X
given by
T=U\cap V
with
U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
and
V\in{\mathsf{RNbhd}}(\phi)
. It holds that
\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(T)=\omega_{\phi}(U)\cap\alpha_{\phi}(V):=M
which is called a
Morse set
. By construction
M\subset{\rm int\,}T
, cf.
KMV-1c
.
The Morse sets are denoted by
{\mathsf{Morse}}(\phi)
which is a bounded, meet-semilattice with binary operation
M\wedge M^{\prime}:=\mbox{\rm Inv}_{\phi}(M\cap M^{\prime})
. The Morse neighborhoods are denoted by
{\mathsf{MNbhd}}(\phi)
and form a bounded, meet-semilattice with intersection as binary operation. Both
\emptyset
and
\omega_{\phi}(X)
are neutral elements.
As before
\mbox{\rm Inv}\colon{\mathsf{MNbhd}}\Rightarrow{\mathsf{Morse}}
is a stable
{\mathbf{MLat}}
-structure, where
{\mathbf{C}}={\mathbf{MLat}}
is the category of bounded, meet-semilattices. The triple
({\mathsf{Morse}},{\mathsf{MNbhd}},\mbox{\rm Inv})
is a
{\mathbf{MLat}}
-continuation frame and by the general theory in Section
4
we obtain the
{\mathbf{MLat}}
-étalé space
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Morse}}]
of Morse sets.

By the same token we can treat the Conley form as natural transformation

{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}\colon{\mathsf{Att}}\times{\mathsf{Att}}\Longrightarrow{\mathsf{Morse}},

where the functor
{\mathsf{Morse}}
assigns the bounded, meet-semilattice of Morse sets to
\phi
. By Proposition
4

this leads to
a continuous operation

\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}]\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]\bullet\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Morse}}]\quad\bigl((\phi,A)(\phi,A^{\prime})\bigr)\mapstochar\rightarrow\bigl(\phi,{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}(A,A^{\prime})\bigr).

The map
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}]
will play a role in setting up the appropriate algebraic construction for sheaf cohomology.

A variation on the Conley form is the
symmetric Conley form
which is defined as follows:

(A,A^{\prime})\mapstochar\rightarrow{\mathsf{C}}^{*}(A,A^{\prime}):={\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}(A\cup A^{\prime},A\wedge A^{\prime})=(A\cap A^{\prime*})\cup(A^{\prime}\cap A^{*}).

For the symmetric Conley form we use the following notation:
(A,A^{\prime})\mapstochar\rightarrow A+A^{\prime}
.

Remark 17

The range of the symmetric Conley form is the same as for the standard Conley form. Indeed, if
A^{\prime}\subset A
then
{\mathsf{C}}^{*}(A,A^{\prime})={\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}(A,A^{\prime})
. For any pair of attractor
A,A^{\prime}
absorption implies that
{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}(A,A^{\prime})={\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}(A,A\wedge A^{\prime})
which shows that the Conley form can always be determined from nested pairs, in which case the standard and symmetric Conley forms coincide.

6.3
The algebra of attractors

In this section we take a closer look at the algebraic structure of attractors.
Algebraic structures and in particular (abelian) group structures are important for the (co)homological theory of sheaves.
Our starting point is the lattice of attractors
{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
of a fixed dynamical system
\phi
, which is a bounded, distributive lattice.
Before treating the lattice of attractors we first consider bounded, distributive lattices from a more general point of view.

Let
({\mathsf{L}},\wedge,\vee,0,1)
be bounded, distributive lattice. Then, by the Priestley representation theorem,
{\mathsf{L}}
is isomorphic to the lattice
{\mathcal{O}}^{\rm clp}({\mathsf{\Sigma}}{\mathsf{L}})
of clopen downsets in the ordered topological space
{\mathsf{\Sigma}}{\mathsf{L}}
, the spectrum of
{\mathsf{L}}
, whose points are the prime ideals in
{\mathsf{L}}
and whose topology is the Priestley topology. The latter is a zero-dimensional, compact Hausdorff space. The Boolean algebra
{\mathsf{B}}{\mathsf{L}}
of clopen sets in
{\mathsf{\Sigma}}{\mathsf{L}}
is called the
Booleanization
, or
minimal Boolean extension
of
{\mathsf{L}}
, and
j\colon{\mathsf{L}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{B}}{\mathsf{L}}
is a lattice-embedding given by
j(a)=\{I\in{\mathsf{\Sigma}}{\mathsf{L}}~|~a\not\in I\}
. The Priestley topology is generated by the basis consisting of elements

j(a)\smallsetminus j(b)
,
a,b\in{\mathsf{L}}
.
This construction is functorial; we have the
Booleanization functor

{\mathsf{B}}\colon{\mathbf{BDLat}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{Bool}}
.

Boolean algebras can be given the structure of a ring, i.e. given a Boolean algebra
({\mathsf{B}},\wedge,\vee,^{c},0,1)
define

a+b:=(a\wedge b^{c})\vee(b\wedge a^{c})\quad\hbox{(symmetric difference) and }\quad a\cdot b:=a\wedge b.

Then,
({\mathsf{B}},+,-,0,1)
is a commutative, idempotent ring (idempotency with respect to multiplication). One retrieves the Boolean algebra structure via
a\vee b=a+b+a\cdot b
. We can formulate this as a faithful functor
{\mathsf{I}}\colon{\mathbf{Bool}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}
from the category of Boolean algebras to the category of rings.

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{BDLat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Bool}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Ring}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{B}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{I}}}

Define the ring obtained from Booleanization of
{\mathsf{L}}
as the
(Boolean) lattice ring
of
{\mathsf{L}}
:

{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{L}}:=({\mathsf{I}}\circ{\mathsf{B}})({\mathsf{L}})

(16)

the composition is also denoted by
{\mathsf{R}}:={\mathsf{I}}\circ{\mathsf{B}}
.
This is the natural way to give an abelian structure to a bounded distributive lattice
{\mathsf{L}}
.
We note that
{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{L}}
is in general not free as additive
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-module (vector space), nor as multiplicative monoid.
Since
{\mathsf{L}}
may be regarded as a (commutative) monoid with respect to
\wedge
