we can use the monoid ring construction, cf.
Lang
;
Bergman
;
Tarizadeh
, to define the
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-algebra

{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{L}}
, which is a free
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-module (vector space).
The elements of
{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{L}}
are finite formal sums
\sum_{i}a_{i}
,
a_{i}\in{\mathsf{L}}
, with the additional requirement that
2a=a+a=0
.
Multiplication is given by
a\cdot b:=a\wedge b
. We refer to
{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{L}}
as the
lattice algebra
of
{\mathsf{L}}
which was first introduced in the context of minimal Boolean extension by MacNeille
MacNeille
.
The lattice algebra
{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{L}}
is clearly a Boolean ring as is the lattice ring.
The
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-monoid ring construction defines a covariant functor

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{BDLat}}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{Ring}}.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathds{Z}_{2}}}

The analog of the homomorphism
j\colon{\mathsf{L}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{B}}{\mathsf{L}}
is now given by the ring homomorphism:

j\colon{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{L}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{L}},\qquad j\Bigl(\sum_{i}a_{i}\Bigr):=\sum_{i}j(a_{i})=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}.

By definition
j(a\wedge b)=j(a)\cap j(b)
, which makes
j
an algebra homomorphism.
The image of the generators of
{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{L}}
in
{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{L}}
are downsets in
{\mathsf{\Sigma}}{\mathsf{L}}
and via the induced
\vee
operation the lattice
{\mathsf{L}}
can be retrieved.
By construction

\displaystyle j(a)+j(b)

\displaystyle=\bigl(j(a)\cup j(b)\bigr)\smallsetminus\bigl(j(a)\cap j(b)\bigr)=j(a\vee b)\smallsetminus j(a\wedge b)

\displaystyle={\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(a\vee b,a\wedge b),

When
b\subset a
, then
j(a)+j(b)={\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(a,b)
and thus the sums
j(a)+j(b)
exhaust the range of the Conley form
{\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}\colon{\mathsf{L}}\times{\mathsf{L}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{L}}
.
We define the set
{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{L}}:=\bigl\{{\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(a,b)~|~a,b\in{\mathsf{L}}\bigr\}
as the
convexity monoid
: for
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{L}}
we have
\sigma\cdot\sigma^{\prime}={\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(a,b)\cap{\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(a^{\prime},b^{\prime})={\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(a\wedge a^{\prime},b\vee b^{\prime})\in{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{L}}

and
\sigma\cdot 1={\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(a,b)\cap{\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(1,0)={\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(a\wedge 1,b\vee 0)={\mathsf{C}}^{\sigma}(a,b)=\sigma
. Clearly the embedding

i\colon{\mathsf{C}}{\mathsf{L}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{L}}

is a monoid homomorphism.

Lemma 8

The ring homomorphism
j\colon{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{L}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{L}}
is surjective.

Proof

Let
\gamma\in{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{L}}
, then by a property of the Priestley topology we can express

\gamma
as finite union of the form:
\gamma=\bigcup_{i}\alpha_{i}\smallsetminus\alpha_{i}^{\prime}
,
with
\alpha_{i}=j(a_{i})
,
\alpha_{i}^{\prime}=j(a^{\prime}_{i})
and
a_{i},a^{\prime}_{i}\in{\mathsf{L}}
.
The objective is to prove that
\gamma
is in the range of
j
.
Consider
\alpha\smallsetminus\beta\cup\gamma\smallsetminus\delta
. We may assume without loss of generality that
\beta\subset\alpha
and
\delta\subset\gamma
. Indeed, use
\alpha\smallsetminus\beta=\alpha\smallsetminus(\alpha\cap\beta)
.
Therefore,
\alpha\smallsetminus\beta\cup\gamma\smallsetminus\delta=(\alpha+\beta)\cup(\gamma+\delta)
, and

\displaystyle(\alpha+\beta)\cup(\gamma+\delta)

\displaystyle=\alpha+\beta+\gamma+\delta+(\alpha+\beta)\cap(\gamma+\delta)

\displaystyle=\alpha+\beta+\gamma+\delta+(\alpha\cap\gamma)+(\alpha\cap\delta)+(\beta\cap\gamma)+(\beta\cap\delta),

which corresponds to a sum of
j
-images of elements in
{\mathsf{L}}
.
We conclude that
\gamma=\bigcup_{i}\alpha_{i}\smallsetminus\alpha_{i}^{\prime}=\sum_{k}\tilde{\alpha}_{k}=\sum_{k}j(\tilde{a}_{k})
,
\tilde{a}_{k}\in{\mathsf{L}}
, which proves that
\gamma
is in the range of
j
.

We now have the following short exact sequence:

\begin{diagram}

(17)

and since
the kernel
\ker j
is an ideal in
{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{L}}
the first isomorphism theorem for rings yields

{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{L}}\cong\frac{{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{L}}}{\ker j},

where the isomorphism is given
\sum_{i}a_{i}+\ker j\mapstochar\rightarrow\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}
.
If we regard
j
as a module (vector space) homomorphism then
both
\ker j
and
{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
are free
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
-modules.
The ideal
\ker j
can be characterized as follows.

Lemma 9

\ker j
is the ideal freely generated by elements of the form
a\vee b+a+b+a\cdot b
.

Proof

For an element
a\vee b+a+b+a\cdot b
we have that

\displaystyle j(a\vee b+a+b+a\cdot b)

\displaystyle=j(a\vee b)+j(a)+j(b)+j(a\cdot b)

\displaystyle=j(a)\cup j(b)+j(a)+j(b)+j(a)\cap j(b)

\displaystyle=2\bigl(j(a)\cup j(b)\bigr)=\emptyset,

which proves that finite sums of elements of the form
a\vee b+a+b+a\cdot b

are contained in
\ker j
.
Let
j\Bigl(\sum_{i}a_{i}\Bigr)=\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=\emptyset
, then the sum must have an even number of terms. We can rearrange the sequence to a filtration
\alpha_{1}^{\prime}\subset\cdots\subset\alpha^{\prime}_{2m}
such that
\sum_{i}\alpha_{i}=\sum_{i}\alpha^{\prime}_{i}=\emptyset
. Consequently
\alpha^{\prime}_{2k-1}+\alpha^{\prime}_{2k}=\emptyset
for
k=1,\cdots,m
, i.e.
\alpha^{\prime}_{2k-1}=\alpha^{\prime}_{2k}
for all
k
.
In order to have distinct elements mapping to
\alpha^{\prime}_{2j-1}=\alpha^{\prime}_{2j}

we have

j(b_{k}+c_{k})=\alpha^{\prime}_{2k-1}=\alpha^{\prime}_{2k}=j(b_{k}\vee c_{k}+b_{k}\cdot c_{k}),

which proves that element in
\ker j
is contained in the set of formal sums
generated by terms of the form
a\vee b+a+b+a\cdot b
.

Let us return to the lattice of attractors
{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
. Define the
attractor ring
of a dynamical system
\phi
as
{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi):=({\mathsf{I}}\circ{\mathsf{B}})\bigl({\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)\bigr)
as the Boolean lattice ring of
{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
. This is the natural way to give an abelian structure to the attractors of a dynamical system. Via the monoid ring construction we obtain
the algebra
{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
which
is called the
free attractor ring
over
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.

7
Continuation sheaves

From an abstract continuation frame we have shown how to build an étalé space which encodes the continuation of the unstable structure of interest. This étalé space
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
connects the topology of the base space to the algebraic structure of
{\mathsf{G}}
. To study this connection, we shift our attention to the sheaves of sections generated by the étalé spaces of continuation frames. While sheaves and étalé spaces are equivalent from a categorical viewpoint, the theory of sheaves contributes a rich algebraic toolkit to our study of continuation. Perhaps most prominent is the idea of sheaf cohomology.

Definition 3

Let
({\mathsf{G}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{w}})
be a continuation frame and let
(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}],\pi)

be the associated étalé space over
\mathbf{D}
. A section in
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
over an open set
\mathrm{\Omega}
in
\mathbf{D}
is a continuous map
\sigma\colon\mathrm{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
such that

\pi\circ\sigma=\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits.

The set of all sections over
\mathrm{\Omega}
is denoted by
{S}^{\mathsf{G}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
. The set of global sections,
{S}^{\mathsf{G}}(\mathbf{D}),
is also written as
\mathrm{\Gamma}({S}^{\mathsf{G}})
.

The presheaf

{S}^{\mathsf{G}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbf{D})\rightarrow{\mathbf{Set}},

where
{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbf{D})
is the category of open sets in
\mathbf{D}
, is in fact a sheaf over
\mathbf{D}
and is
called the
sheaf of sections
,
cf.
(
Tenni
, Sect. 2.2C)
.
A stalk of the sheaf
{S}^{\mathsf{G}}
at
\phi\in\mathbf{D}
is the object
{\mathsf{G}}(\phi)
.
By considering sections in
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
we obtain the sheaf of sections
{S}^{\mathsf{E}}
and
stalks in
{S}^{\mathsf{E}}
are denoted by
{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)
, cf.
(
Tenni
, Prop. 3.6)
.

Remark 18

There are multiple equivalent ways to define stalks. The sheaf-theoretic definition is a direct limit
{S}^{\mathsf{G}}_{\phi}:=\varinjlim{S}^{\mathsf{G}}(U)
over open neighborhoods
U
of a point
\phi
. Equivalently, for an étalé space
\pi\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]\rightarrow\mathbf{D}
the stalk at
\phi
can be defined as
\pi^{-1}(\phi)
. In our setting, we make the identification between
\pi^{-1}(\phi)
and
{\mathsf{G}}(\phi)
.

Lemma 10

Let
({\mathsf{G}},{\mathsf{E}},{\mathsf{w}})
be a continuation frame and let

\sigma\colon\mathrm{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
be a map with property that
\pi\circ\sigma=\mathop{\rm id}\nolimits
on
\mathrm{\Omega}
(open).
Then,
\sigma
is a section in
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
if and only if for every
\phi\in\mathrm{\Omega}
there exists an open neighborhood
\mathrm{\Omega}_{0}\subset\mathrm{\Omega}
of
\phi
and
U\in{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)
, such that

{\left.\kern-1.2pt\sigma\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Omega}_{0}}}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Omega}_{0}}}
.

Proof

This follows immediately from the definition of sheaves.

Sections therefore act locally like
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]
. Following this intuition, observe that
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]
is a section in
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{G}}]
over
\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{E}};U]
.

The above lemma means we only need to verify that a candidate section locally agrees with
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{w}};U]
for a
particular

U\in{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)
for some
\phi\in{\mathsf{ob}}(\mathbf{D})
, rather than all such
U
.
By the same token sections
\sigma\colon\mathrm{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{E}}]
are given locally by
\mathrm{\Theta}[{\mathsf{id}};U]
, i.e.
\sigma(\phi)=(\phi,U)
.

From the construction of the sheaves
{S}^{\mathsf{E}}
and
{S}^{\mathsf{G}}
we have the following property of the natural transformation
{\mathsf{w}}
:

\begin{diagram}

where
{\mathsf{w}}(\mathrm{\Omega})\colon{S}^{\mathsf{E}}(\mathrm{\Omega})\rightarrow{S}^{\mathsf{G}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
is defined by
\sigma\mapstochar\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{w}}]\circ\sigma
,
\mathrm{\Omega}
open, and similarly for
\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Omega}
.
The maps
\rho_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime},\mathrm{\Omega}}
are the restriction maps.
The latter defines a
morphism of sheaves

{\mathsf{w}}\colon{S}^{\mathsf{E}}\rightarrow{S}^{\mathsf{G}}
.
Since
{\mathsf{w}}
yields the
stalkwise surjections
{\mathsf{w}}_{\phi}\colon{\mathsf{E}}(\phi)\twoheadrightarrow{\mathsf{G}}(\phi)
, we say that the morphism
{\mathsf{w}}\colon{S}^{\mathsf{E}}\rightarrow{S}^{\mathsf{G}}
is surjective.

7.1
Attractor sheaves

In Section
6.1
we constructed étalé spaces in various categories such as bounded, distributive lattices. The above sheaf of sections construction creates sheaves with values in these same categories.
For example, the
{\mathbf{C}}
-structure
({\mathsf{Att}},{\mathsf{ANbhd}},\omega)

yields
the étalé morphism
\mathrm{\Pi}[\omega]\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}}]\twoheadrightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
and the
{\mathbf{BDLat}}
-valued sheaves

{S}^{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X))\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
and

{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X))\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}.

Hence, we obtain the following morphism of sheaves

\omega\colon{S}^{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\twoheadrightarrow{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}

that assigns to every section
\sigma\colon\mathrm{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}}]
the section

\mathrm{\Pi}[\omega](\sigma)\colon\mathrm{\Omega}\rightarrow\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
.
The sheaf
{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
is called the
attractor lattice sheaf

over
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
.

Similarly, we have the morphism of sheaves

\alpha\colon{S}^{\mathsf{RNbhd}}\twoheadrightarrow{S}^{\mathsf{Rep}},

where
{S}^{\mathsf{Rep}}
is the
repeller lattice sheaf
.
Duality between
{\mathsf{Att}}
and
{\mathsf{Rep}},
as well as between
{\mathsf{ANbhd}}
and
{\mathsf{RNbhd}},
yields the following commutative diagram of sheaves:

\begin{diagram}

The Conley form on étalé spaces in Section
6.2
gives rise to the
{\mathbf{MLat}}
-valued sheaf
{S}^{\mathsf{Morse}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X))\rightarrow{\mathbf{MLat}}
.

For
{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
and
{S}^{\mathsf{Morse}}
, lattice-valued and meet lattice-valued sheaves respectively,
we need to
construct a suitable abelian structure in order to define their sheaf cohomology.
In general, let
{\mathbf{C}}
be a concrete category, and let
{\mathsf{K}}\colon{\mathbf{C}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}
a covariant functor. Moreover, let
{S}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbf{D})\rightarrow{\mathbf{C}}
be a
{\mathbf{C}}
-valued sheaf over a topological category
\mathbf{D}
.
For every open set
\mathrm{\Omega}
in
\mathbf{D}
we have the ring
{\mathsf{K}}{S}(\mathrm{\Omega})
and the ring-valued presheaf

{\mathsf{K}}{S}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbf{D})\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}.

Via the sheafification functor
{}^{\#}\colon{\mathbf{PrSh}}_{\mathbf{C}}(X)\rightarrow{\mathbf{Sh}}_{\mathbf{C}}(X)
, we then obtain the sheaf

{K}:=\bigl({\mathsf{K}}{S}\bigr)^{\#}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathbf{D})\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}.

In Section
6.3
we consider two functors that take values in the category of rings: the
Boolean ring functor

{\mathsf{R}}={\mathsf{I}}\circ{\mathsf{B}}\colon{\mathbf{BDLat}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}
and
the
monoid ring functor

{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\colon{\mathbf{Monoid}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}
. In the case of the sheaf of attractors we obtain the abelian sheaves:

{\mathcal{A}}:=\bigl({\mathsf{R}}{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}\bigr)^{\#}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X))\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}},

(18)

and

{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}:=\bigl({\mathds{Z}_{2}}{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}\bigr)^{\#}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X))\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}},

(19)

which are called the
attractor sheaf
and the
free attractor sheaf

over
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
respectively.

Remark 19

Similar constructions can be applied to sheaves in other dynamical contexts.
The construction via the functor
{\mathds{Z}_{2}}\colon{\mathbf{Monoid}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}
works for all of the above examples since both bounded, distributive lattices and semilattices compose subcategories of the category of (commutative) monoids. Of particular interest is the

free Morse sheaf

{\mathcal{M}\!\!\mathcal{o}\!\mathcal{r}\!\mathcal{s}\!\mathcal{e}}:=\bigl({\mathds{Z}_{2}}{S}^{\mathsf{Morse}}\bigr)^{\#}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X))\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}.

Remark 20

The short exact sequence in (
17
) for
{\mathsf{Att}}
yields:

\begin{diagram}

(20)

where the stalks

{\mathcal{A}}_{\phi}={\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)\quad\text{and}\quad{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}_{\phi}={\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)

are the attractor ring at
\phi
and the free attractor ring over
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
at
\phi
respectively. We define this to be the
fundamental short exact sequence of the attractor sheaf
.
The fundamental exact sequence allows us
to relate the sheaves
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{M}\!\!\mathcal{o}\!\mathcal{r}\!\mathcal{s}\!\mathcal{e}}
. The generators of
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}
and the ring structure of
{\mathcal{A}}
recover the attractor lattice sheaf
{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
.

Remark 21

An alternative way to define the sheaves
{\mathcal{A}}
and
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}
is a direct definition via étalé spaces. In the case of
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}
we define an étalé space
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{Att}}]
using the stable
{\mathbf{C}}
-structure
\bigl({\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{Att}},{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}},{\mathds{Z}_{2}}(\omega)\bigr)
via the monoid ring functor. The stability follows from the fact that stability is preserved under free sums. We obtain the étalé space
\pi\colon\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{Att}}]\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
and the associated sheaf of sections
{S}^{{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
. It holds that

{S}^{{\mathds{Z}_{2}}{\mathsf{Att}}}\cong{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}
.
For the Boolean ring functor it is more involved to prove that
\bigl({\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{Att}},{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}},{\mathsf{R}}(\omega)\bigr)
is a continuation frame, but
{S}^{{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{Att}}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}
.

7.2
Finite sublattice and Morse representation sheaves

Following Lemma
7
, we also have an
{\mathbf{Lat}}
-valued étalé space
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}]
, encoding the continuation of finite sublattices of attractors. As earlier, we can consider the corresponding sheaf of sections
{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)\rightarrow{\mathbf{Lat}}
. For an open set
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
, a section in
{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
assigns to each dynamical system
\phi\in\mathrm{\Omega}
a finite sublattice of
{\mathsf{A}}\subset{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
. The lattice operations on
{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
, on stalks, send two finite sublattices to their intersection or the smallest sublattice containing both.
This yields the following question concerning the structure of the sheaf
{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
:

Can we view sections of
{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
as a lattice of sections of
{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
?

This is not always possible, see Example
2
.

To understand this structure one needs to be able to relate the sheaves
{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
and
{S}^{{\mathsf{Att}}}
. Define the following étalé space on
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
:

\mathrm{\Pi}:=\bigl\{(\phi,{\mathsf{A}},A)\in\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}]\bullet\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]:A\in{\mathsf{A}}\bigr\}.

The projection from
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}]\bullet\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]

remains a surjective local homeomorphism when restricted to the subspace
\mathrm{\Pi}
.
There is a commutative diagram of restriction maps for étalé spaces:

\begin{diagram}

Denote the sheaf of sections associated to the étalé space
\mathrm{\Pi}
by

{E}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}({\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X))\rightarrow{\mathbf{Set}}.

A section of
{E}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
traces out the continuation of a finite sublattice of attractors, as well as a specific attractor in that sublattice. From the diagram, there are two morphisms:

q\colon{E}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}\rightarrow{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}\quad\quad r\colon{E}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}\rightarrow{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}},

which restricts sections to their attractor and finite sublattice components respectively. For an open set
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
and a section
\nu\in{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
, we can consider the set
r_{\mathrm{\Omega}}^{-1}(\nu)
consisting of sections of
{E}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
which agree with
\nu
on their finite sublattice component. This set has a bounded distributive lattice structure, defined on the attractor component. Suppose
\sigma,\sigma^{\prime}\in r_{\mathrm{\Omega}}^{-1}(\nu)
:

\sigma(\phi)=(\nu(\phi),A),\quad\sigma^{\prime}(\phi)=(\nu(\phi),A^{\prime}),\quad\quad(\sigma\wedge\sigma^{\prime})(\phi):=(\nu(\phi),A\wedge A^{\prime}).

The meet operation is defined similarly. We can then pass this lattice through
q
, and achieve a bounded distributive lattice of sections of
{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
:

q_{\mathrm{\Omega}}\colon r_{\mathrm{\Omega}}^{-1}(\nu)\subset{E}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}(\mathrm{\Omega})\rightarrow{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}(\mathrm{\Omega})

We make the following observations:

•

For any section
\sigma\in q_{\mathrm{\Omega}}\bigl(r_{\mathrm{\Omega}}^{-1}(\nu)\bigr)
, with
\sigma(\phi)=(\phi,A)
, we have that
A\in{\mathsf{A}}
, where
\nu(\phi)=(\phi,{\mathsf{A}})
. In other words, the value of
\sigma
at
\phi
is contained in the value of
\nu
at
\phi
.

•

Composing the stalk restriction map
\rho_{\phi}\colon{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}(\mathrm{\Omega})\rightarrow{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
yields the composite lattice homomorphism

f_{\mathrm{\Omega},\phi}\colon q_{\mathrm{\Omega}}\bigl(r_{\mathrm{\Omega}}^{-1}(\nu)\bigr)\rightarrow{\mathsf{A}}\subset{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi),

where
\nu(\phi)=(\phi,{\mathsf{A}})
.

•

If
f_{\mathrm{\Omega},\phi}
is surjective at every
\phi\in\mathrm{\Omega}
, we retrieve
\nu
from these sections:

\nu(\phi)=\bigl(\phi,\{A_{\sigma}\}\bigr),\quad\textnormal{where}\quad{\sigma\in q_{\mathrm{\Omega}}\bigl(r_{\mathrm{\Omega}}^{-1}(\nu)\bigr)},\quad\sigma(\phi)=(\phi,A_{\sigma}).

Proposition 5

Let
\nu\in{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
for some open set
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
and let
\phi\in\mathrm{\Omega}
. Then, there is an open neighborhood
\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}
of
\phi
such that
f_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}}
defined by
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\nu\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}}}
is surjective for all
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}
.

Proof

By Lemma
10

\nu
yields a neighborhood
\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}
of
\phi
upon which
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\nu\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}}}={\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathrm{\Theta}[\bm{\mathsf{\omega}};{\mathsf{N}}]\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}}}
for some
{\mathsf{N}}\in{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
. For each
U\in{\mathsf{N}}
, we have a section
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathrm{\Theta}[\omega;U]\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}}}\in{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}(\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime})
. Indeed, we can define the following section in
{E}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}(\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime})
:

\phi\mapstochar\rightarrow\bigl(\phi,\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\phi}({\mathsf{N}}),\omega_{\phi}(U)\bigr)

which maps to
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathrm{\Theta}[\omega;U]\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}}}
under
q
, and therefore
{\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathrm{\Theta}[\omega;U]\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}}}\in q_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}}\bigl(r_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}}^{-1}(\nu)\bigr)
. Let
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}
, and
A\in{\mathsf{A}}
, where
\nu(\phi^{\prime})=(\phi^{\prime},{\mathsf{A}})
. Then
A=\omega_{\psi}(U)
for some
U\in{\mathsf{N}}
, since
\bm{\mathsf{\omega}}_{\psi}({\mathsf{N}})={\mathsf{A}}
. Moreover, since
f_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt\mathrm{\Theta}[\omega;U]\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}}}\big)=A
for arbitrary choices of
\phi^{\prime}
and
A
, the proof is complete.

Proposition
5
justifies that
locally
a section in
{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
may be interpreted as a finite distributive lattice of sections in
{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
. We will investigate when this interpretation extends globally at a later stage.

Dually, we can consider the continuation frame
\bigl({\mathsf{MRepr}},{\mathsf{MTess}},\bm{\mathsf{\Delta}}\bigr)
, which defines the
{\mathbf{Lat}}
-valued
Morse representation sheaf

{S}^{\mathsf{MRepr}}
, cf. Sect.
5.2
. Applying Proposition
3
to the natural transformation
\bm{\mu}\colon{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{MRepr}}
with stable extension
\bm{\tau}\colon{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\rightarrow{\mathsf{MTess}}
yields a sheaf isomorphism

\bm{\mu}\colon{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}\rightarrow{S}^{\mathsf{MRepr}}.

The lattice structure of
{S}^{\mathsf{MRepr}}
allows common coarsings and refinements of Morse representations:
let
\sigma_{\mathsf{M}},\sigma_{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}\in{S}^{\mathsf{MRepr}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
, then

\sigma_{\mathsf{M}}\vee\sigma_{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}\in{S}^{\mathsf{MRepr}}(\mathrm{\Omega})\quad\hbox{and}\quad\sigma_{\mathsf{M}}\wedge\sigma_{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}\in{S}^{\mathsf{MRepr}}(\mathrm{\Omega}),

the
common coarsening
and
common refinement
of Morse representations continuations respectively.
The binary operations are defined in the sheaf
{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
via

\bm{\mu}^{-1}(\sigma_{\mathsf{M}})\wedge\bm{\mu}^{-1}(\sigma_{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime})\quad\hbox{and}\quad\bm{\mu}^{-1}(\sigma_{\mathsf{M}})\vee\bm{\mu}^{-1}(\sigma_{\mathsf{M}}^{\prime}),

respectively.
We can dualize the earlier theory for
{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
to describe sections of
{S}^{\mathsf{MRepr}}
. For a section
\zeta\in{\mathsf{MRepr}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
, there is a corresponding section
\nu:=\bm{\mu}(\zeta)\in{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
. We again have
f_{\mathrm{\Omega},\phi}\colon q_{\mathrm{\Omega}}(r^{-1}_{\mathrm{\Omega}}(\nu))\rightarrow{\mathsf{A}}
where
\nu(\phi)=(\phi,{\mathsf{A}})
. Suppose
q_{\mathrm{\Omega}}(r^{-1}_{\mathrm{\Omega}}(\nu))
is finite. We can dualize to achieve:

g_{\mathrm{\Omega},\phi}\colon{\mathsf{M}}({\mathsf{A}})\rightarrow{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathrm{\Omega}},

where
{\mathsf{P}}_{\mathrm{\Omega}}
denotes the poset of join-irreducible elements of
q_{\mathrm{\Omega}}(r^{-1}_{\mathrm{\Omega}}(\nu))
. The map
g_{\mathrm{\Omega},\phi}
composes the isomorphism between
{\mathsf{J}}({\mathsf{A}})
, the join-irreducible elements of
{\mathsf{A}}
, and
{\mathsf{M}}({\mathsf{A}})
with the dual of
f_{\mathrm{\Omega},\phi}
. The Morse representation
{\mathsf{M}}({\mathsf{A}})
is exactly the value of
\zeta
at
\phi
, in other words,
\zeta(\phi)=(\phi,{\mathsf{M}}({\mathsf{A}}))
. If the lattice morphism
f_{\mathrm{\Omega},\phi}
is surjective, then
g_{\mathrm{\Omega},\phi}
is an embedding and thus a
Morse decomposition
, cf.
(
KMV-1c
, Def. 7)
.
Thus we get an analogous statement to Proposition
5
.

Corollary 3

Let
\zeta\in{S}^{{\mathsf{MRepr}}}(\mathrm{\Omega})
for an open
\mathrm{\Omega}\subset{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
such that
q_{\mathrm{\Omega}}(r^{-1}_{\mathrm{\Omega}}(\bm{\mu}_{\mathrm{\Omega}}(\zeta)))
is finite, and
\phi\in\mathrm{\Omega}
. Then there is an open neighborhood
\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime}
of
\phi
such that
g_{\mathrm{\Omega}^{\prime},\phi^{\prime}}
is a Morse decomposition for all
\phi^{\prime}\in\mathrm{\Omega^{\prime}}
.

8
Parameter spaces and pullbacks

In this section we discuss continuation frames for parametrized families of dynamical systems
and how the associated sheaves can be constructed.

8.1
Parametrized dynamical systems

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}
be a topological space.
In keeping with the spirit of the paper we keep the conditions mild but in practical situations
\mathrm{\Lambda}
is a CW-space.

Definition 4

Let
X
be a compact topological space.
A
parametrized dynamical system over
\mathrm{\Lambda}
on
X
is a continuous map

{\bm{\phi}}\colon{\mathbb{T}}\times X\times\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow X
such that

\phi^{\lambda}:={\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot,\lambda)\in{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
for all
\lambda\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
.

The category of dynamical systems
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
is a function space equipped with the compact-open topology.
For a parametrized dynamical system
{\bm{\phi}}
we define the
transpose

{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
by

{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\lambda)=\phi^{\lambda}:={\bm{\phi}}(\cdot,\cdot,\lambda).

The transpose
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
is a continuous map without additional assumptions on the topological spaces
\mathrm{\Lambda}
and
X
, cf. Appendix
D
.

For the continuation frame
({\mathsf{Att}},{\mathsf{ANbhd}},\omega)
on
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
a parametrized dynamical system yields a pullback étalé space on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
:

{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]:=\bigl\{(\lambda,\phi,A)\in\mathrm{\Lambda}\times\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]~|~{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\lambda)=\pi(\phi,A)=\phi\bigr\},

i.e. the follows diagram commutes

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Lambda}}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda,\phi,A)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\phi,A)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces(\lambda,\phi,A)\mapstochar\rightarrow\lambda}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\pi}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}}

where
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
is the pullback in the category of topological spaces, cf.
(
Bredon
, Sect. I.3)
. From
(
Borceux
, Prop. 2.4.9)
it follow that
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]\rightarrow\mathrm{\Lambda}
is an étalé space over
\mathrm{\Lambda}
.
The binary operations on
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
can be verified to be continuous on the inverse image étalé space.
As before we obtain the following
{\mathbf{BDLat}}
-valued pullback sheaf

{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathrm{\Lambda})\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}},

as the sheaf of sections of
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
.
Applying the boolean ring functor
{\mathsf{R}}
to the sheaf of sections yields a ring valued sheaf:

{\mathcal{A}}^{{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}}:=\bigl({\mathsf{R}}{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{S}^{{\mathsf{Att}}}\bigr)^{\#}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathrm{\Lambda})\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}.

The
ringed space

(\mathrm{\Lambda},{\mathcal{A}}^{{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}})
encodes the continuation data of attractors for the parametrized dynamical system. Similarly, for the Monoid ring functor
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}\cdot
we obtain:

{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}}:=\bigl({\mathbb{Z}}_{2}{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{S}^{{\mathsf{Att}}}\bigr)^{\#}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathrm{\Lambda})\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}.

where the multiplication is inherited from the monoidal structure of
{\mathsf{Att}}
, cf. Section
6.3
. We are now in the setting of sheaf cohomology. Since the category of sheaves of abelian groups has enough injectives, the
i
th sheaf cohomology groups
may be defined as the right derived functors of the global section functor. A more direct and detailed construction can be found in
Bredon
. We apply these, and their relative versions, to the sheaves
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
and
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
. Theorem
8.1
and the later sections will show the cohomology groups
H^{i}(\mathrm{\Lambda},{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
and
H^{i}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
are algebraic invariants which can detect bifurcations.

Remark 22

The sheaf
{\mathcal{A}}^{{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}}
can be alternatively defined as follows. The Boolean ring functor yields the étalé space
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{Att}}]
and the associated sheaf of sections

{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{S}^{{\mathsf{R}}{\mathsf{Att}}}\cong{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{\mathcal{A}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}},

which defines the sheaf as the pullback sheaf with respect to
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
.

8.2
Conjugate dynamical systems and homeomorphic étalé spaces

We start off with the basic notion of conjugacy in dynamical systems.

Definition 5

Let
X
and
Y
be compact topological spaces, and
let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
and
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},Y)
be parametrized dynamical systems.
A
conjugacy
between
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
and
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is a continuous map
h\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\times X\rightarrow Y
and a continuous reparametrization
\tau\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\times{\mathbb{T}}\times X\rightarrow{\mathbb{T}}
, such that

(i)

h^{\lambda}\times\tau^{\lambda}:=h(\lambda,\cdot)\times\tau(\lambda,\cdot,\cdot)

is a conjugacy in
{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi^{\lambda},\psi^{\lambda})
for all
\lambda\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
;

(ii)

h^{\lambda}(X_{i})=Y_{i}
uniformly for all
\lambda\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
, where
X_{i}
and
Y_{i}
are the connected components of
X
and
Y
respectively.

If a conjugacy
h
exists, then
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
and
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
are said to be
conjugate parametrized dynamical systems
.

Remark 23

Assumption (ii) is always satisfied pointwise for
\lambda
by
appropriately indexing the components of
X
and
Y
. The uniformity in the above definition is not guaranteed since no restrictions on the topology of
\mathrm{\Lambda}
are required. For specific topologies on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
condition (ii) may be superfluous.

Remark 24

One may
also consider quasiconjugacies between parametrized dynamical systems over
\mathrm{\Lambda}
.

Since
h^{\lambda}
is a conjugacy we know from Remark
6
that
the push-forward
U^{\lambda}\mapstochar\rightarrow h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda})
is an attracting neighborhood for
\psi_{\lambda}
and similarly, the push-forward
A^{\lambda}\mapstochar\rightarrow h^{\lambda}(A^{\lambda})

is an attractor for
\psi_{\lambda}
.

Lemma 11

The following diagram commutes:

\begin{diagram}

Proof

Indeed, the maps from above we have
U^{\lambda}\mapstochar\rightarrow h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda})\mapstochar\rightarrow\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\bigl(h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda})\bigr)
. From below yields
U^{\lambda}\mapstochar\rightarrow A^{\lambda}=\omega_{\phi^{\lambda}}(U^{\lambda})\mapstochar\rightarrow h^{\lambda}(A^{\lambda})
.
Since
h
is a conjugacy it follows from Remark
6
that Lemma
1
applies to both
h^{\lambda}
and
(h^{\lambda})^{-1}
. This gives:

\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\bigl(h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda})\bigr)=\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\bigl(h^{\lambda}\bigl(\omega_{\phi^{\lambda}}(U^{\lambda})\bigr)\bigr)=\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\bigl(h^{\lambda}(A^{\lambda})\bigr)=h^{\lambda}(A^{\lambda}),

(21)

which proves commutativity.

Lemma
11

holds for all
\lambda\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
and which provides stalkwise isomorphisms between the associated sheaves of attractors. This however does not give isomorphic sheaves necessarily!

Theorem 8.1
(Conjugacy Invariance Theorem)

Let
X
,
Y
be
compact metric spaces. Suppose
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
and
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},Y)
are conjugate parametrized dynamical systems.
Then, the étalé spaces
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
and
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
are homeomorphic.

Proof

From Lemma
11
we have the following commutative diagram of maps:

\begin{diagram}

where
h_{*}
is defined by
(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},A^{\lambda})\mapstochar\rightarrow h_{*}(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},A^{\lambda}):=\bigl(\lambda,\psi^{\lambda},h^{\lambda}(A^{\lambda})\bigr)
.
It is sufficient to show continuity, since if
h_{*}
is continuous, then
h_{*}
is a local homeomorphism, in which case
h_{*}^{-1}
is a also a local homeomorphism (
h_{*}
is a bijection), cf.
(
Borceux
, Prop. 2.4.8)
. This proves that
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
and
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
are homeomorphic.

In order to prove continuity we argue as follows. Consider the following commutative diagram

\begin{diagram}

where
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]=\bigl\{\lambda~|~U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi^{\lambda})\bigr\}
and

{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}[\omega;U](\lambda)=\bigl(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},\omega_{\phi^{\lambda}}(U)\bigr)
.
Let
D_{0}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
be an open neighborhood of
\lambda_{0}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
and let

{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}\big[\omega;h^{\lambda_{0}}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\big](D_{0})=\biggl\{\Bigl(\lambda,\psi^{\lambda},\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\bigl(h^{\lambda_{0}}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\bigr)\Bigr)~|~\lambda\in D_{0}\biggr\}

be an open neighborhood of
h_{*}\bigl(\lambda_{0},\phi^{\lambda},A^{\lambda_{0}}\bigr)=\bigl(\lambda_{0},\psi^{\lambda},h^{\lambda_{0}}(A^{\lambda_{0}})\bigr)
in
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
for some compact
U^{\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi^{\lambda_{0}})
.
In order to establish continuity we seek a neighborhood
D_{0}^{\prime}\subset D_{0}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}

such that

\displaystyle h_{*}\Bigl({\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}[\omega;U^{\lambda_{0}}]

\displaystyle(D_{0}^{\prime})\Bigr)=\bigg\{\Big(\lambda,\psi^{\lambda},h^{\lambda}\bigl(\omega_{\phi^{\lambda}}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\bigr)\Big)~|~\lambda\in D_{0}^{\prime}\bigg\}

\displaystyle=\bigg\{\Big(\lambda,\psi^{\lambda},\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\big(h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\big)\Big)~|~\lambda\in D_{0}^{\prime}\bigg\}\subset{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}\big[\omega;h^{\lambda_{0}}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\big](D_{0}),

where the second equality follows from Lemma
11
, Eqn. (
21
).
This is equivalent to saying

\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\big(h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\bigr)=\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\bigl(h^{\lambda_{0}}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\bigr),\quad\forall\lambda\in D_{0}^{\prime}.

For notational convenience we write

U:=h^{\lambda_{0}}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\psi^{\lambda_{0}}),\quad\hbox{and}\quad A=h^{\lambda_{0}}(A^{\lambda_{0}})=\omega_{\psi^{\lambda_{0}}}(U^{\prime})\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi^{\lambda_{0}}).

We rephrase the above condition as:

\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\big(h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\bigr)=\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}(U),\quad\forall\lambda\in D_{0}^{\prime}.

(22)

For
U^{\lambda_{0}}=\varnothing
, or for
U^{\lambda_{0}}=\bigsqcup_{i}X_{i}\subset X
, any union
of connected components of
X
, Eqn. (
22
) is satisfied by the uniform conjugacy condition in Defn.
5
(ii), cf. Remark
23
.
For the remainder of the proof we assume
U^{\lambda_{0}}\not=\varnothing
and

U^{\lambda_{0}}\neq\bigsqcup_{i}X_{i}
, for all unions
of connected components of
X
.
Therefore, we may carry out the arguments for the components
U^{\lambda_{0}}_{i}=U^{\lambda_{0}}\cap X_{i}\neq\varnothing,X_{i}
.

Choose a compact attracting neighborhood
U^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\psi^{\lambda_{0}})
such that
U^{\prime}\subset{\rm int\,}U

and
\omega_{\psi^{\lambda_{0}}}(U^{\prime})=A
. Indeed, since
A
is an attractor
{\rm cl}\,U^{c}\cap A=\varnothing
, cf.
(
KMV-1a
, Lemma 3.23)
. Therefore there exists open sets
N,N^{\prime}
such
that
A\subset N
,
{\rm cl}\,U^{c}\subset N^{\prime}
and
N\cap N^{\prime}=\varnothing
. As a matter of fact

{\rm cl}\,N\cap N^{\prime}=\varnothing
. Define
U^{\prime}={\rm cl}\,N
. By construction
A^{*}\subset U^{c}\subset{\rm cl}\,U^{c}\subset N^{\prime}
and thus
U^{\prime}\cap A^{*}=\varnothing
which proves that (i)
\omega_{\psi^{\lambda_{0}}}(U^{\prime})=A
, (ii)
A\subset N\subset U^{\prime}
, (iii)
U^{\prime}={\rm cl}\,N\subset N^{\prime c}\subset\bigl({\rm cl}\,U^{c}\bigr)^{c}={\rm int\,}U
,
and thus

U^{\prime}
is an attracting neighborhood satisfying the properties stated above, cf.
(
KMV-1a
, Lemma 3.21)
. From the fact that
U\neq\bigsqcup_{i}Y_{i}
, a union of components,
it follows that
{\rm int\,}U\subsetneq U
. Thus by Property (iii)
there exists a
\delta_{1}>0
such that
B_{\delta_{1}}(U^{\prime})\subset U
and therefore
d_{\mathsf{H}}(U,U^{\prime})\geq\delta_{1}>0
, where
d_{\mathsf{H}}
is the Hausdorff metric on the space
{\mathsf{H}}(X)
of compact subsets of
X
.

By the same token we can choose a compact repelling neighborhood
V\in{\mathsf{RNbhd}}(\psi^{\lambda_{0}})

such that
V\cap U=\varnothing
and
\omega_{\psi^{\lambda_{0}}}(V^{c})=A
.
Indeed, repeat the above arguments starting with
U\cap A^{*}=\varnothing
.
V
is compact, so there exists a
\delta_{2}>0
such that
d_{\mathsf{H}}(U,V)\geq\delta_{2}>0
.

Since,
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}\big[\omega;U\big]
,
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}\big[\omega;U^{\prime}\big]

and
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}\big[\omega;V^{c}\big]
define local sections in
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
over
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U]
,
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};U^{\prime}]
and
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Phi}[{\mathsf{ANbhd}};V^{c}]
respectively, and since

{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}\big[\omega;U\big](\lambda_{0})={\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}\big[\omega;U^{\prime}\big](\lambda_{0})={\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Theta}\big[\omega;V^{c}\big](\lambda_{0})

there exists an open set
E_{0}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
on which three sections coincide, i.e.

B^{\lambda}:=\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}(U)=\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}(U^{\prime})=\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}(V^{c}),\quad\forall\lambda\in E_{0},

and
B^{\lambda}\subset{\rm int\,}U
,
B^{\lambda}\subset{\rm int\,}U^{\prime}
and
B^{\lambda}\subset{\rm int\,}V^{c}
for all
\lambda\in E_{0}
.

Let
\widetilde{U}
be any compact neighborhood such that
d_{\mathsf{H}}(U,\widetilde{U})<\delta=\min\{\delta_{1},\delta_{2}\}/2
and let
\lambda\in E_{0}
.
Then,

B^{\lambda}\subset U^{\prime}\subset\widetilde{U},\qquad\widetilde{U}\cap(B^{\lambda})^{*}\subset\widetilde{U}\cap V=\varnothing,

which by
(
KMV-1a
, Lemma 3.21)
implies that
\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}(\widetilde{U})=B^{\lambda}
for all
\lambda\in E_{0}
.

Finally, using the continuity of
h^{\lambda}_{\mathsf{H}}
in Lemma
18
, choose an open sets
D_{0}^{\prime}\subset E_{0}\cap D_{0}
such that

d_{\mathsf{H}}\bigl(h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda_{0}}),U\bigr)<\delta
for all
\lambda\in D_{0}^{\prime}
.
By the previous we choose
\widetilde{U}=h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda_{0}})
which proves that

\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}\bigl(h^{\lambda}(U^{\lambda_{0}})\bigr)=B^{\lambda}=\omega_{\psi^{\lambda}}(U),\quad\forall\lambda\in D_{0}^{\prime},

establishing (
22
) and thereby the theorem.

Remark 25

The condition that the spaces
X
and
Y
are compact metric spaces is used at several places in the proof and in particular for using the Hausdorff metric. The characterizations of attracting and repelling neighborhoods via attractors and dual repellers at least works in compact Hausdorff spaces.

Theorem
8.1
can be extended to other structures. Since
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
is homeomorphic (as a sheaf of sets) to
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Rep}}]
, we can get a homeomorphism between
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Rep}}]
and
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Rep}}]
. There is the following commutative diagram for Morse sets:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]\bullet{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]\bullet{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Morse}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Morse}}]}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\mathrm{\Lambda}}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}{[}{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}{]}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Pi}{[}{\mathsf{C}}_{\mathsf{Att}}{]}}

where the top horizontal map is given by

(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},A),(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},A^{\prime})\mapstochar\rightarrow(\lambda,\psi^{\lambda},h^{\lambda}(A)),(\lambda,\psi^{\lambda},h^{\lambda}(A^{\prime}))

and the bottom horizontal map is given by

(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},M)\mapstochar\rightarrow(\lambda,\psi^{\lambda},h^{\lambda}(M)),

which, using a similar argument to Propositon
3
, establishes that the étalé spaces
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Morse}}]
and
{\bm{{\psi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Morse}}]
are homeomorphic.

Corollary 4

Let
X
and
Y
be homeomorphic compact metric spaces and let
{\mathsf{Att}}_{X}
and
{\mathsf{Att}}_{Y}
be the attractor functors on
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
and
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},Y)
respectively. Then, the étalé spaces
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}_{X}]
and

\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}_{Y}]
are homeomorphic.

Proof

Let
h\colon X\rightarrow Y
be a homeomorphism and let
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
. Then,
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is the identity map. The map
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},Y)
is defined as follows:
\mathrm{\Lambda}\ni\phi\mapstochar\rightarrow h\circ\phi\circ h^{-1}=\psi
. Then,

h\bigl(\phi_{t}(x)\bigr)=h\Bigl(\phi_{t}\bigl(h^{-1}(y)\bigr)\Bigr)=\psi_{t}(y)=\psi_{t}\bigl(h(x)\bigr),

which proves that
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
and
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
are conjugate parametrized dynamical systems.

9
Bifurcations and sheaf cohomology

Sheaves attach both local and global data to a topological space. In our setting of continuation, they encode how dynamical structures vary with parameter values on open sets. Oftentimes, given an open cover of the topological space, one can glue together the local information on each element of the cover to obtain global information.
However, sometimes local information fails to extend globally. Sheaf cohomology, which can be viewed as a generalization of singular cohomology, is a powerful tool for studying this. An interpretation for singular cohomology groups is that they constitute obstructions to a topological space being contractible. Sheaf cohomology generalizes this by representing barriers for local sections to extend to global sections.

One can always solve an attractor’s continuation
locally
using an attracting neighborhood. But this problem is sometimes impossible
globally
.
Sheaf cohomology provides a framework for quantifying when and how this occurs. Together with the conjugacy invariance theorem, this will build an algebraic invariant for parametrized dynamical systems, which can be used to study bifurcations.

Recall that a parametrized dynamical system on a topological space
\mathrm{\Lambda}
is a continuous
map
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
such that
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\lambda)\colon{\mathbb{T}}\times X\rightarrow X
is a dynamical system for all
\lambda\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
. In principal
\mathrm{\Lambda}
may be
{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
but in practice simpler topological spaces for
\mathrm{\Lambda}
are used. In this section, to utilize Theorem
8.1
,
we assume
X
is a compact metric space.

Definition 6

A parametrized dynamical system
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
is
stable
at a point
\lambda_{0}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
if there exists an open neighborhood
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\ni\lambda_{0}
such that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}
is conjugate to the constant parametrization
{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
, given by
\lambda\mapstochar\rightarrow{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\lambda_{0})
for all
\lambda\in\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
. If
\lambda_{0}
is not stable, it is called a
bifurcation point
.
A parametrized dynamical system
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is
stable
on a subset
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
if it is stable at every point in
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
.

If a parametrized dynamical system
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
is conjugate to the constant parametrization
{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
it is called
uniformly stable
.

In general stability of a parametrized dynamical system does not imply uniform stability. For instance if
\mathrm{\Lambda}
is not connected
then
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
need not be conjugate to a fixed constant system
{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}
.
This example indicates that stability does not imply uniform stability in general if
\mathrm{\Lambda}

is disconnected. See Example
1
for an counter example with a connected space
\mathrm{\Lambda}
.

9.1
Locally constant sheaves

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
be a parametrized dynamical system.
From the previous we have the induced attractor sheaf and free attractor sheaf over
\mathrm{\Lambda}
:

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathrm{\Lambda})\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}},\qquad{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathrm{\Lambda})\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ring}}.

The ringed spaces
(\mathrm{\Lambda},{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
and
(\mathrm{\Lambda},{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
encode the continuation data of attractors for the parametrized dynamical system. At a later stage we also include the attracting neighborhood sheaf and free attracting neighborhood sheaf
{\mathcal{N}}
and
{\mathcal{A}\!\!\mathcal{N}\!\mathcal{b}\!\mathcal{h}\!\mathcal{d}}
respectively.

Recall that for an abelian group

{\mathsf{E}}\in{\mathbf{Ab}}
the presheaf
{E}\colon{\mathcal{O}}(\mathrm{\Lambda})\rightarrow{\mathbf{Ab}}
defined by
{E}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}):=\bigl\{\sigma\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathsf{E}}~~\text{constant}\bigr\}
,
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
open, is called the
constant presheaf
over
\mathrm{\Lambda}
with values in
{\mathsf{E}}
. The sheafification
\underline{{\mathsf{E}}}:={E}^{\#}
is called the
constant sheaf
over
\Lambda
with values in
{\mathsf{E}}
. The constant sheaf can be characterized as the sheaf of locally constant functions with values in
{\mathsf{E}}
, i.e.

\underline{{\mathsf{E}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime})=\bigl\{\sigma\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathsf{E}}~~\text{locally constant}\bigr\},\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda},~~\text{open}.

If we equip
{\mathsf{E}}
with the discrete topology then such functions are continuous functions
\sigma\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\rightarrow{\mathsf{E}}
. This corresponds to the sheaf of section of the étalé space
\mathrm{\Lambda}\times{\mathsf{E}}
, with
{\mathsf{E}}
equipped with the discrete topology, cf.
(
Tenni
, Sect. 2.4)
,
(
Wedhorn
, Ex. 3.31 and 3.40)
.
If
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
is open and connected then
\underline{{\mathsf{E}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime})\cong{\mathsf{E}}
. For an open set whose connected components are open then
\underline{{\mathsf{E}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime})
is isomorphic to a direct product of copies of
{\mathsf{E}}
, one for each connected component, cf.
(
Hartshorne
, Ex. 1.0.3)
.
An abelian sheaf
{F}
is called
locally constant
is there exists an open covering
\{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{i}\}

of
\mathrm{\Lambda}
such that
{F}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{i}}
is a constant sheaf for all
i
. This is equivalent to saying that every point allows a neighborhood
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
such that
{F}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}
is constant, cf.
(
Bergman
, Defn. I.1.9)
.
Locally constant sheaves are sheaves of sections of covering spaces,
(
Wedhorn
, Ex. 3.41)
.

Lemma 12

Let
{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
be a constant parametrization. Then, the sheaves
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}

and
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}
are constant sheaves.

Proof

The pullback étalé space
{\bm{{\theta^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]
is given by

{\bm{{\theta^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Att}}]\cong\mathrm{\Lambda}\times{\mathsf{A}},

where
{\mathsf{A}}={\mathsf{Att}}(\phi^{\lambda_{0}})
, for some
\lambda_{0}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
,
is given the discrete topology. Therefore the sheaf of sections

{\bm{{\theta^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{S}^{{\mathsf{Att}}}
is a constant sheaf.
Consequently,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}
and
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}
are also constant sheaves.

Lemma 13

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
be stable. Then, the sheaves
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}

and
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
are locally constant sheaves.

Proof

Pick a point
\lambda_{0}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
.
Since

{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is stable
there exists a neighborhood
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\ni\lambda_{0}
such that

{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda^{\prime}}}
is conjugate to the constant parametrization.
By the Conjugacy Invariance Theorem in
8.1
we have that

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}
as sheaves. The latter is a constant sheaf over
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
and therefore
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}
is a constant sheaf over
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
by definition.
We conclude that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is locally constant. The same applies to
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
.

Remark 26

If
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is uniformly stable then
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is conjugate to a constant parametrization
{\bm{{\theta_{\bm{*}}}}}
on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
. The associated étalé spaces are homemorphic by Theorem

8.1
and thus the sheaves
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}

and
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
are constant sheaves is this case.

Example 1

Let
X
be the 2-point compactification of the line and consider the following family of differential equations

\dot{x}=\sin(x+\lambda),\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},~~\lambda\in{\mathbb{S}}^{1}={\mathbb{R}}/2\pi{\mathbb{Z}}.

The above system defines a 1-parameter family of flows
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{R}},X)

of flows on
X
over parameter space
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{S}}^{1}
. Via the conjugacy
x\mapstochar\rightarrow x-\lambda
we conclude that
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is stable and thus the attractor sheaf
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is a locally constant sheaf as indicated by Lemma
13
. Since
\pm\infty
are not attractors, the only global sections in
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
are
\varnothing
and
X
. The stalks of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
are infinite complete, atomic Boolean algebras which proves that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is not a constant sheaf.

The above example shows that even if
\mathrm{\Lambda}
is connected, then a stable system need not be uniformly stable. Indeed,
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
in Example
1
allows a conjugacy over
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{S}}^{1}
, then
the attractor sheaf
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is constant which contradicts above statement that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is locally constant but not constant.

Example 2

Define a vector field restricted to the compact subset
[-2,2]\times[-2,2]
of
{\mathbb{R}}^{2}
:

F(x,y)=(-x(x+1)(x-1),-y).

We can rotate the vector field with a parameter
\theta
:

F_{\theta}(x,y)=R_{-\theta}F(R_{\theta}(x,y)),

where
R_{\theta}
denotes the rotation matrix of angle
\theta
. Since
F_{\pi}(x,y)=F_{0}(x,y)
, gluing at
0
and
\pi
(not 2
\pi
) gives us a parametrized dynamical system
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon{\mathbb{S}}^{1}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{R}},[0,2]\times[0,2])
by integrating the vector field.
The invariant set
[-1,1]\times\{0\}
undergoes a half-twist over
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
. There are only three global sections of
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
:

\theta\mapstochar\rightarrow\emptyset,\quad\theta\mapstochar\rightarrow R_{\theta}(\{(1,0),(-1,0)\}),\quad\theta\mapstochar\rightarrow R_{\theta}([-1,1]\times\{0\}).

Alas, each stalk is a five element lattice and
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
is connected, so
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
is not the constant sheaf. Additionally, the five element attractor lattice is a global section in
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{S}^{{\mathrm{sub_{F}}}{\mathsf{Att}}}
, but cannot be represented as a collection of global sections of
{\bm{{\phi^{-1}_{\bm{*}}}}}{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
.

Figure 2
:
An illustration of example
2
. The vector field at the cross section is rotated over the parameter space
{\mathbb{S}}^{1}
. The system is stable, so the attractor sheaf is locally constant. However, it is impossible to continue either attracting fixed point globally.

As pointed out above, a locally constant sheaf is the sheaf of sections of a covering space.
With additional conditions on
\mathrm{\Lambda}

such sheaves may be constant sheaves.

Proposition 6
(cf.
Iversen
, Prop. 4.20 and
Wedhorn
, Prop. 7.5)

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}
be a simply connected and locally path connected topological space,
and let
{F}
be a locally constant sheaf of rings on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
.
Then,
{F}
is a constant sheaf.

The same statement holds for contractible spaces
\mathrm{\Lambda}
, cf.
(
Schapira
, Exer. II.4)
.
We can apply the above proposition to the attractor sheaf
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
and free attractor sheaf
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
for simple parametrized systems
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
.

Corollary 5

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
be stable and
let
\mathrm{\Lambda}
be a simply connected and locally path connected topological space. Then,
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}

and
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
are constant sheaves.

For constant sheaves the sheaf cohomology can be related to singular cohomology which is a useful tool in our treatment of bifurcations.

Proposition 7
(cf.
Notzeb
, Thm. 9)

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}
be a locally contractible topological space (
(
Spanier
, p. 57)
),
and let
{\mathsf{R}}
be an arbitrary ring. If
\underline{{\mathsf{R}}}
denotes the constant sheaf with values in
{\mathsf{R}}
, then
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};\underline{{\mathsf{R}}})\cong H^{k}_{\rm sing}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathsf{R}})
for all
k
.

If we combine Lemma
13
, Corollary
5
and Proposition
7

we obtain a result that determines the sheaf cohomology of the attractor sheaves for simple parametrized dynamical systems.

Corollary 6

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)
be stable and
let
\mathrm{\Lambda}
be a locally contractible and simply connected topological space. Then,

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong H^{k}_{\rm sing}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}_{\lambda_{0}}),\quad\forall k,

where
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}_{\lambda_{0}}\in{\mathbf{Ring}}
is a stalk at any
\lambda_{0}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
.
A similar statement holds for
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
.

Proof

Lemma
13
implies that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is a constant sheaf.
A locally contractible space is locally simply connected and locally path connected,
but not necessarily simply connected.
In combination with the condition of simple connectedness we can combine Corollary
5
and Proposition
7
, which completes the proof.

9.2
Sufficient conditions

The statements about sheaf cohomology in Section
9.1
imply the following sufficient condition for bifurcations to exist. The theorems stated for the attractor sheaf
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
can also be stated for the free attractor sheaf
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
.

Theorem 9.1

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}
be both contractible and locally contractible.
Suppose that

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\neq 0,\quad\text{ for some }\quad k>0.

Then, there exist a bifurcation point in
\lambda_{0}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}
.

Proof

Suppose there are no bifurcation points. This implies that
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is stable
which by Corollary
6
implies that

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong H^{k}_{\rm sing}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathsf{R}})
for all
k
(where
{\mathsf{R}}
is isomorphic to a stalk of
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
).
Since
\mathrm{\Lambda}
is contractible, we have that

H^{k}_{\rm sing}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathsf{R}})=0
for all
k>0
.
Combining these statements yields that
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong H^{k}_{\rm sing}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathsf{R}})=0
for all
k>0
, which contradicts the above assumptions.

As we will see in Section
10
the above criterion does not always detect bifurcations.
In order to get a more in depth look into local bifucations we consider its relative sheaf cohomology for
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
.
We use the following lemma about long exact sequences in sheaf cohomology.

Lemma 14

Let
{F}
be a sheaf of rings on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
and let

\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{i}}\mathrm{\Lambda}
.
Assume that the induced homomorhisms

i_{*}^{k}\colon H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})\rightarrow H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})
are isomorphisms for all
k\geq 0
.
Then,

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong 0,\quad\forall k\geq 0.

Proof

For triple
(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime},\varnothing){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{i}}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\varnothing){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{j}}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime})
we have the long exact sequence,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{2}}

For the exactness of the maps and the isomorphisms
i_{*}^{k}
we have:

\ker j_{*}^{0}=\textnormal{im~}\delta^{0}=0
, which proves that
j_{*}^{0}
is injective. Furthermore,
since
i_{*}^{0}
is an isomorphism we have

\ker i_{*}^{0}=0=\textnormal{im~}j_{*}^{0}
and thus
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong 0
.
The remaining relative homology groups are determined as follows:
\ker\delta^{1}=\textnormal{im~}i_{*}^{0}=H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})
. Therefore,
\ker j_{*}^{1}=\textnormal{im~}\delta^{1}=0
, which shows that
j_{*}^{1}
is injective. Furthermore,

\ker i_{*}^{1}=0=\textnormal{im~}j_{*}^{1}
, consequently
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong 0
.
The same argument can be repeated now for all other
k
.

As an immediate consequence of the long exact sequence we have the following corollary
if we apply Lemma
14
to the attractor sheaf
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
.

Corollary 7

Suppose
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\neq 0
for some
k
. Then, there exist
k_{0}\geq 0
for which
the inclusion
i
does not imply an isomorphism
i_{*}^{k_{0}}\colon H^{k_{0}}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\rightarrow H^{k_{0}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
.

The relative sheaf cohomology can be used to formulate an analogous criterion as Theorem
9.1
.

Theorem 9.2

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}
be both contractible and locally contractible, and let

\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
be a deformation retract of
\mathrm{\Lambda}

with
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
stable on
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
.
Suppose that

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\neq 0,\quad\text{ for some }\quad k\geq 0.

Then, there exist a bifurcation point in
\lambda_{0}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}\smallsetminus\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
.

Proof

Suppose there are no bifurcation points in
\mathrm{\Lambda}\smallsetminus\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
. This implies that
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is stable on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
.
Since
\mathrm{\Lambda}
is contractible and locally contractible, it is simply connected and locally path connected. It follows from Proposition
5
that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is a constant sheaf on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
. Since
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
is a deformation retract of
\mathrm{\Lambda}
, the same holds for
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}\cong{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
. This implies that
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
. By Corollary
6
, since
i^{k}_{*}\colon H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathsf{R}})\rightarrow H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathsf{R}})
is an isomorphism for all
k
, we have that
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0
for all
k\geq 1
. Combining these statements gives
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
for all
k
.
This implies by Lemma
14
that

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0
for all
k
, which
contradicts the assumption that
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\neq 0
for some
k
.
Therefore,
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is not stable on
\mathrm{\Lambda}\smallsetminus\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
and there exists a bifurcation point

\lambda_{0}\in\mathrm{\Lambda}\smallsetminus\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
.

10
Examples of one-parameter bifurcations

In this section we discuss a number of standard one-parameter bifurcations such as a saddle-node bifurcation and a pitchfork bifurcation. We will also examine bifurcation at multiple bifurcation points. The objective is to show that sheaf cohomology picks up bifurcarions. At a later stage we will discuss the more practical side of computing sheaf cohomology from limited data.

10.1
One-parameter bifurcations at a single parameter value

In this subsection we list three fundamental bifurcations in one-parameter systems. We apply the above results to compute the sheaf cohomology and to compare the criteria.

For example if
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{R}}
or
\mathrm{\Lambda}=I
, a bounded interval, then the above theorem applies. This is of interest for one-parameter bifurcations.
The following lemma addresses the case where
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
has one bifurcation point on
{\mathbb{R}}
, which will assist in computations.

Lemma 15

Let
{F}
be a sheaf of rings on
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{R}}
, such that
{F}
is a constant sheaf on both
(-\infty,\lambda_{0})
and
(\lambda_{0},\infty)
for some
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
. Then,
{F}
is acyclic, i.e.
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},{F})=0
for all
k\geq 1
.

Proof

Let
\epsilon>0
and let
B_{\epsilon}
denote the interval
(\lambda_{0}-\epsilon,\lambda_{0}+\epsilon)
. There is a restriction cohomomorphism
r\colon{F}\leadsto{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}
. We will show this induces an isomorphism of cohomology:

r^{*}\colon H^{*}({\mathbb{R}};{F})\rightarrow H^{*}\big(B_{\epsilon};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}\big).

(23)

First we address global sections. Because
{F}
is constant on
(-\infty,\lambda_{0})
and
(\lambda_{0},\infty)
, sections in
\mathrm{\Gamma}({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}})
extend uniquely to sections in
\mathrm{\Gamma}({F})
. Thus,
r_{0}^{*}\colon\mathrm{\Gamma}({F})\rightarrow\mathrm{\Gamma}({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}})
is an isomorphism.
For
k>1
,
H^{k}({\mathbb{R}};{F})
and
H^{k}\big(B_{\epsilon};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}\big)
vanish, since intervals have covering dimension 1, cf.
(
Schapira
, Lemma 2.7.3 and Proposition 3.2.2)
. So the maps

r_{k}^{*}\colon H^{k}({\mathbb{R}};{F})\rightarrow H^{k}\big(B_{\epsilon};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}\big)

are trivially isomorphisms. Now we consider
k=1
. Let
{\mathbb{R}}^{*}={\mathbb{R}}\smallsetminus\{\lambda_{0}\}
, so that
B_{\epsilon}
and
{\mathbb{R}}^{*}
form a cover of
{\mathbb{R}}
. Note that
{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}
is locally constant, with vanishing higher cohomology groups. There is a Mayer-Vietoris exact sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Gamma}({F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}\big)\oplus\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}({\mathbb{R}};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\big(B_{\epsilon};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}\big)\oplus H^{1}\big({\mathbb{R}}^{*};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\big(B_{\epsilon}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{*};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}

Since
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}})
and
H^{1}(B_{\epsilon}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{*};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}})
vanish the sequence simplifies to:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Gamma}({F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}\big)\oplus\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}({\mathbb{R}};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(B_{\epsilon};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces r_{1}^{*}}

The map
\beta
is surjective, since the restriction from
\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}\big)
to
\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}\cap{\mathbb{R}}^{*}}}\big)
is surjective. Following the sequence yields
\textnormal{Im}\,\delta=\ker r_{1}^{*}=0
.
\textnormal{im~}\,r_{1}^{*}=\ker 0=H^{1}(B_{\epsilon};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}})
, so
r_{1}^{*}
is also surjective.
This implies that the restriction cohomomorphism
r\colon{F}\leadsto{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}
induces an isomorphism on cohomology and establishes (
23
).
Indeed, for
\epsilon^{\prime}<\epsilon
, the restriction cohomomorphism from
{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}
to
{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon^{\prime}}}}
is an isomorphism, again giving an isomorphism of cohomology. So,

H^{*}\big({\mathbb{R}};{F}\big)\approx\varinjlim_{\epsilon>0}H^{*}\big(B_{\epsilon};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}\big).

We can compute the limit using
(
Bredon
, Theorem 10.6)
:

\varinjlim_{\epsilon>0}H^{*}\big(B_{\epsilon};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{B_{\epsilon}}}\big)\approx H^{*}\big(\{\lambda_{0}\};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\{\lambda_{0}\}}}\big).

Since
{\left.\kern-1.2pt{F}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\{\lambda_{0}\}}}
is flasque (restriction maps are surjective), it is acyclic, completing the proof.

The same results hold for
\mathrm{\Lambda}=I
, a bounded, or semi-bounded interval.
In the applications below
\mathrm{\Lambda}
is typically the real line.

Lemma 16

Let
{F}
be a sheaf of rings on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
and let
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}{\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{i}}\mathrm{\Lambda}
.
Assume that
{F}
and
{F}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}
are acyclic.
If

(i)

i_{*}^{0}\colon H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
is injective, then
\textnormal{im~}i_{*}^{0}\cong H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})
and

H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong\frac{H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}{\textnormal{im~}i_{*}^{0}},\quad\text{and}\quad H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong 0,\quad\text{for}\quad k\neq 1;

(ii)

i_{*}^{0}\colon H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\rightarrow H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
is surjective, then

H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong\ker i_{*}^{0},\quad\text{and}\quad H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong 0,\quad\text{for}\quad k\neq 0.

Proof

As before for triple
(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime},\varnothing){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{i}}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\varnothing){\mathrel{\rightarrow}^{j}}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime})
we have the long exact sequence,

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\cdots.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{2}}

Since, by Lemma
15
,
{F}
is acyclic we obtain the truncated sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{1}}

(24)

Since
i_{*}^{0}
is injective and thus
\ker i_{*}^{0}=0=\textnormal{im~}j_{*}^{0}
.
Moreover,
\ker j_{*}^{0}=\textnormal{im~}\delta^{0}=0
, which implies that
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong 0
. Consequently, we have the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{1}}

from which the result for
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}{F})
follows.
The cohomology
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}{F})\cong 0
, for
k\geq 2
follows from Lemma
14
, which completes the proof of (i).

As for (ii) we have the truncated exact sequence in (
24
). Now
i_{*}^{0}
is surjective
which implies that
\ker\delta^{1}=\textnormal{im~}i_{*}^{0}=H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})
. Therefore,

\ker j_{*}^{1}=\textnormal{im~}\delta^{1}=0
and thus
j_{*}^{1}
is injective. Consequently,
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}{F})\cong 0
. We now have the short exact sequence

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}

which implies that
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{F})\cong\ker i_{*}^{0}
. The relative homology for
k\geq 1
follows from Lemma
14
.

Remark 27

The sheaf cohomology groups of the abelian attractor sheaf can be equipped with a cup product from the ring structure of the sheaf. We leave cup product computations and their interpretation for later work.

10.1.1
The pitchfork bifurcation

Consider a parametrized dynamical system on
X={\mathbb{R}}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
, the 2-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
, experiencing a pitchfork bifurcation, cf. Figure
3
.
The parametrized flow is defined via the differential equation

\dot{x}=\lambda x-x^{3},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},~\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}}.

Before the bifurcation point at
\lambda_{0}=0

there are two repelling fixed points at
+\infty
and
-\infty
and
a single attracting fixed point at
x=0
. After
\lambda_{0}=0
, there are two additional attracting
fixed points
x=\pm x_{\lambda}
and
x=0
has changed to a repelling fixed point.
We fix a parametrization:

{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X),

where
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{R}}
is parameter space,
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}^{+}
is the time space and
X
is the 2-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
.

\infty
-\infty
0
\mathrm{\Lambda}
X
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
U

Figure 3:
In the pitchfork bifurcation, the section on
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}\subset\mathrm{\Lambda}
defined by
\sigma(\lambda)=\big(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},\omega_{\phi^{\lambda}}(U)\big)
fails to extend globally.

Lemma 17

The global sections of the abelian attractor sheaf for the normal form pitchfork bifurcation are
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\bigl({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
.

Proof

First, consider the global sections of the attractor lattice sheaf
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}^{-1}{S}^{\mathsf{Att}}
, which are uniquely characterized by their value at the bifurcation point
\lambda=0
. Assigning
\emptyset
and
X
to every parameter value gives the bottom and top elements of
\mathrm{\Gamma}({\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}^{-1}{S}^{\mathsf{Att}})
. Then, we have the section which assigns
\lambda\leq 0
the sole attracting fixed point
x=0
, and
\lambda>0
the interval
[-x_{\lambda},x_{\lambda}]
. Finally, there are two sections which are
[-\infty,0]
and
[0,\infty]
for
\lambda\leq 0
, but
[-\infty,x_{\lambda}]
and
[-x_{\lambda},\infty]
for
\lambda>0
respectively. This yields a five element lattice with three join irreducible elements: the latter three sections. Applying the boolean ring functor to this lattice yields
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
.

In later results we will omit the above types of computations.

Proposition 8

Let
