\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}:=[a,\infty)
. If
a>0
, then
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{2}
for
k=1
, and
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0
otherwise. When
a\leq 0
, all relative cohomology groups vanish.

Proof

Lemma
17
gives
\mathrm{\Gamma}\bigl({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
. For
a>0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{5}_{2}
, and injectivity of
i_{*}^{0}
,
which by Lemma
16
(i) yields
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{2}_{2}
.
For
a\leq 0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
,
which implies
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0
.
Since by Lemma
15
both
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
and
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}
2
2

2

If
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
does not contain a bifurcation point then acyclicity follows from the fact that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}
is a constant sheaf on a contractible manifold.
are acyclic,
the higher order relative cohomology groups vanish by Lemma
16
(i).

Proposition 9

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}:=(-\infty,a]
. Then,
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0
for all
k

and for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof

Note that
\mathrm{\Gamma}\bigl({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}^{3}_{2}

for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
(the same computations as in Lemma
17
apply to
\Lambda^{\prime}
). Therefore,
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})
for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
and thus by Lemma
16
(i)
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0

for all
k
.

Theorem 10.1

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
be a parametrized dynamical system over
\mathrm{\Lambda}
conjugate to the above canonical parametrization
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
for the pitchfork bifurcation. Then,

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}~\text{~ is acyclic and ~}~H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}.

Moreover,
there exists a value
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

H^{k}\bigl(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a>\lambda_{0};\\
0&\textnormal{if $k\neq 1$ or $a\leq\lambda_{0}$,}\end{cases}

where
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty)
,
Furthermore, for
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}:=(-\infty,a]
, then

H^{k}\bigl(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong 0
for all
k
and for all
a\in{\mathbb{R}}
.

Proof

This follows immediately from Theorem
8.1
, Lemma
15
, and Propositions
8
and
9
.

This theorem can be applied locally in parameter space. If
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{R}};I)
is some parametrized dynamical system such that
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
experiences a pitchfork bifurcation on an open set
U
, then
{\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{U}}
has the above cohomology groups.
Another important observation is that the relative cohomology
in the example below is the same for a local pitchfork bifuraction.

Example 3

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
be a parametrized flow over
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{R}}
on the interval
X=[-1,1]

with a single attracting fixed point at
x=0
for
\lambda\leq 0
.
This system is a semi-flow with
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}^{+}
.
For
\lambda\geq 0
the system undergoes a pitchfork bifurcation with two branches
\pm x_{\lambda}
of attracting fixed points converging to
\pm 1
respectively as
\lambda\rightarrow+\infty
, cf. Figure
3
.
If we repeat the analysis in Propositions
8
and
9

the sheaf cohomology over
\mathrm{\Lambda}
is different:
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is acyclic and
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. On the other hand the relative sheaf cohomologies

H^{k}\bigl({\mathbb{R}},[a,\infty);{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)
and
H^{k}\bigl({\mathbb{R}},(-\infty,a];{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)
are the same.

10.1.2
The saddle-node bifurcation

Consider a parametrized dynamical system on
X={\mathbb{R}}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
, the 2-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
, experiencing a saddle-node bifurcation.
The parametrized flow is defined via the differential equation

\dot{x}=\lambda-x^{2},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},~\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}},

and
+\infty
and
-\infty
are a repelling and attracting fixed point respectively.
Before the bifurcation point at
\lambda_{0}=0
, the entire interval flows from
+\infty
to
-\infty
. After
\lambda_{0}=0
, there is an additional attracting and repelling fixed point.
We fix a parametrization:

{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X),

where
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{R}}
is parameter space,
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}
is the time space and
X
is the 2-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Lemma
15
again shows that the attractor sheaf
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
has vanishing higher order cohomology, but relative cohomology recognizes the bifurcations.

\infty
-\infty
0
\mathrm{\Lambda}
X
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
U

Figure 4:
A saddle-node bifurcation. The section on
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
defined by
\sigma(\lambda)=\big(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},\omega_{\phi^{\lambda}}(U)\big)
fails to extend globally.

Proposition 10

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty)
. If
a>0
, then

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
for
k=1
, and vanishes otherwise. When
a\leq 0
, then
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0
for all
k
.

Proof

The global sections are
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\bigl({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
.
For
a>0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4}
. The injectivity of
i_{*}^{0}
and
Lemma
16
(i) yields
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
For
a\leq 0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
,
which implies
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0
. As before the higher order relative cohomology groups vanish by Lemma
16
(i).

Proposition 11

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=(-\infty,a]
. If
a>0
, then

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0
for all
k
. When
a<0
, then
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and vanishes otherwise.

Proof

As before the global sections are
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\bigl({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
.
For
a>0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
. The injectivity of
i_{*}^{0}
and
Lemma
16
(i) yields
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0
.
For
a<0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}
. The surjectivity of
i_{*}^{0}
and Lemma
16
(ii)
then implies that
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. The higher order relative cohomology groups vanish by Lemma
16
(i) and (ii).

Theorem 10.2

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
be a parametrized dynamical system over
\mathrm{\Lambda}
conjugate to the above canonical parametrization
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
for the saddle-node bifurcation. Then,

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}~\text{~ is acyclic and~}~H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}.

Moreover,
there exists a value
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a>\lambda_{0}\\
0&k\neq 1,\textnormal{or~}a\leq\lambda_{0},\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty),

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=0\textnormal{ and }a<\lambda_{0}\\
0&k\neq 0,\textnormal{or~}a\geq\lambda_{0},\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=(-\infty,a].

Proof

Apply Theorem
8.1
, Lemma
15
and Propositions
10

and
11
.

Remark 28

The generator of
H^{k}\bigl({\mathbb{R}},(-\infty,a];{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)
when
a<{\lambda_{0}}
is the sum of two global sections of attractors: the bottom fixed point and the maximal attractor. The two coincide before the bifurcation point, which leaves their sum zero. Afterwards, however, this corresponds to the Morse set between the top two fixed points. Continuation of this Morse set to the empty set via a global section yields nontrivial relative cohomology.

Example 4

Consider a saddle-node bifurcation in the system described in Figure
4
.
We impose an attracting fixed point at the bottom of Figure
4
, such that we may restrict phase space to a forward-invariant compact interval
X=[1,0]
. Call this parametrized dynamical system
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon{\mathbb{R}}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{R}}^{+},X)
. Lemma
15
again shows that
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
has vanishing higher cohomology. However,
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}
which differs from the above example. The relative cohomology groups are the same as in the above example as is the case for the pitchfork bifurcation.

10.1.3
The transcritical bifurcation

Consider a parametrized dynamical system on
X={\mathbb{R}}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
, the 2-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
, experiencing a transcritical bifurcation.
The parametrized flow is defined via the differential equation

\dot{x}=\lambda x-x^{2},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},~\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}},

and
+\infty
and
-\infty
are a repelling and attracting fixed points respectively.
As before we fix a parametrization:

{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X),

where
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{R}}
is parameter space,
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}
is the time space and
X
is the 2-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Lemma
15
again shows that the attractor sheaf
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
has vanishing higher order cohomology.

\infty
-\infty
0
\mathrm{\Lambda}
X
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
U

Figure 5:
A trans-critical bifurcation. The section on
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
defined by
\sigma(\lambda)=\big(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},\omega_{\phi^{\lambda}}(U)\big)
fails to extend globally.

Proposition 12

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty)
. If
a>0
, then

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
for
k=1
, and vanishes otherwise. When
a\leq 0
, then
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0
for all
k
.

Proof

The global sections are
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\bigl({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
.
For
a>0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4}
. The injectivity of
i_{*}^{0}
and
Lemma
16
(i) yields
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
.
For
a\leq 0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
,
which implies
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0
. As before the higher order relative cohomology groups vanish by Lemma
16
(i).

Proposition 13

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=(-\infty,a]
. If
a\geq 0
, then

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0
for all
k
. When
a<0
, then
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
and vanishes otherwise.

Proof

As before the global sections are
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\bigl({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
.
For
a\geq 0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
. The injectivity of
i_{*}^{0}
and
Lemma
16
(i) yields
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0
.
For
a<0
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4}
. The injectivity of
i_{*}^{0}
and Lemma
16
(i)
then implies that
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. The higher order relative cohomology groups vanish by Lemma
16
(i).

Theorem 10.3

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
be a parametrized dynamical system over
\mathrm{\Lambda}
conjugate to the above canonical parametrization for the transcritical bifurcation. Then,

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}~\text{~ is acyclic and~}~H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}.

Moreover,
there exists a value
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a>\lambda_{0}\\
0&k\neq 1,\textnormal{or~}a\leq\lambda_{0},\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty),

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a<\lambda_{0}\\
0&k\neq 1,\textnormal{or~}a\geq\lambda_{0},\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=(-\infty,a].

Proof

Apply Theorem
8.1
, Lemma
15
and Propositions
10

and
11
.

Remark 29

Note the subtle difference in the relative sheaf cohomology for the saddle-node and transcritical bifurcations. For the latter we only find relative cohomology at
k=1
for different choices of
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
, as for the saddle-node we have cohomology at
k=0
and
k=1
for various choices of
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
.

10.2
One-parameter bifurcations at multiple parameter values

In this subsection we consider a bifurcation that occur at multiple points.

10.2.1
The S-shaped bifurcation

Now we study the S-shaped bifurcation, as in Figure
6
.
Consider a parametrized dynamical system on
X={\mathbb{R}}\cup\{-\infty,\infty\}
, the 2-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
, experiencing an S-shaped bifurcation. The parametrized flow is defined via the differential equation

\dot{x}=\lambda+x-x^{3},\quad x\in{\mathbb{R}},~\lambda\in{\mathbb{R}},

and
+\infty
and
-\infty
are a repelling fixed points.
As before we fix a parametrization:

{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X),

where
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{R}}
is parameter space,
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}
is the time space and
X
is the 2-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
.
Here, there are two bifurcation points at
\lambda_{1}=-1
and
\lambda_{2}=+1
.

\infty
-\infty
0
\mathrm{\Lambda}
X
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
U

Figure 6:
An S-shaped bifurcation. The section on
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}
defined by
\sigma(\lambda)=\big(\lambda,\phi^{\lambda},\omega_{\phi^{\lambda}}(U)\big)
fails to extend globally.

Proposition 14

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is acyclic.

Proof

Pick
{\lambda}_{1}<a<b<{\lambda}_{2}
, such that
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}:=(-\infty,b]
and
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}:=[a,\infty)
cover
{\mathbb{R}}
. Consider the Mayer-Vietoris exact sequence:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Gamma}({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}}}\big)\oplus\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[a,b]}}\big)}

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\big(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}}}\big)\oplus H^{1}\big(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}\big([a,b];{\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[a,b]}}\big)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha^{0}_{*}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{*}^{1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\beta_{*}^{1}}

since
H^{2}({\mathbb{R}};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0
, which uses the fact that intervals have covering dimension 1, cf.
(
Schapira
, Lemma 2.7.3 and Proposition 3.2.2)
.
We can compute the global sections:

\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3},\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}}}\big)\cong\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4},\quad\quad\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{[a,b]}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{5}.

Since
\textnormal{im~}\delta^{0}\cong 0
and
\ker\delta^{0}\cong 0
we have
\ker\alpha_{*}^{0}=\textnormal{im~}\delta^{0}\cong 0
. Consequently,
\textnormal{im~}\alpha_{*}^{0}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
. Similarly,
\ker\beta_{*}^{0}=\textnormal{im~}\alpha_{*}^{0}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
which implies that
\textnormal{im~}\beta_{*}^{0}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{5}
. Furthermore,
\ker\delta^{1}=\textnormal{im~}\beta_{*}^{0}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{5}
and thus
\textnormal{im~}\delta^{1}\cong 0
.
Since
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1},\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}
both contain only one bifurcation point, we can apply Lemma
15
to conclude that
H^{1}\big(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}}}\big)
and
H^{1}\big(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2};{\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}}}\big)
vanish for all
k\geq 1
. Hence,

\textnormal{im~}\delta^{1}=\ker\alpha_{*}^{1}=H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})
, which proves that
H^{1}({\mathbb{R}},{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})
is zero. The remaining sheaf cohomology vanishes due to the dimension restriction on
\mathrm{\Lambda}
.

The S-shaped bifurcation is an example where
{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
and
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
, the attractor sheaf and free attractor sheaf respectively, have differing cohomologies.

Proposition 15

Let
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
be the free attractor sheaf associated to
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
.

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{5}&\textnormal{if }k=0\\
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4}&\textnormal{if }k=1\\
0&\textnormal{if }k\geq 2\end{cases}

\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}=(-\infty,{\lambda}_{2})
and
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}=({\lambda}_{1},\infty)
be an open covering for
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{R}}
. We build the
ordered Čech complex
from this cover:

which in our case is:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{2}_{1,2}-\rho^{1}_{1,2}}

where
\rho^{2}_{1,2}
denotes the restriction map from
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1})
to
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2})
, and
\rho^{2}_{1,2}
from
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2})
. We get cohomology groups from the above chain complex:

\check{H}^{0}(\{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1},\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}\};{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=\ker\delta_{1}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{5},\quad\quad\check{H}^{1}(\{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1},\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}\};{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=\ker\delta_{2}/\textnormal{Im}\delta_{1}\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4},

\check{H}^{k}(\{\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1},\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}\};{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0\text{ for }k>1.

Since
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}
contains no bifurcation points,
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
is locally constant on
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}
and therefore acyclic on
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}
. We now use Leray’s Theorem to determine the sheaf cohomology of
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
from the above Čech cohomology groups, which yields the desired result.

The result of Proposition
15
is an example where Theorem
9.1
applies. The sheaf cohomology of
{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
picks up bifurcations. For the sheaf cohomology of

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
Theorem
9.1
does not apply.

Proposition 16

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty)
.
If
a\in({\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2}]
, then
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
for
k=1
, and vanishes otherwise. When
a\notin({\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2}]
, then
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0
for all
k
.

Proof

We achieve a truncated long exact sequence from Proposition
14
and Proposition
13
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0.}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces j_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces i_{*}^{0}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\delta^{1}}

The map
i_{*}^{0}
is injective and thus
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong 0
.
Lemma
16
then yields:

H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\frac{H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})}{\textnormal{im~}i_{*}^{0}}

Note that
\textnormal{im~}i_{*}^{0}\cong H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=\mathrm{\Gamma}\bigl({\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\bigr)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}
. For
b\in({\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2}]
, we have
H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=\mathrm{\Gamma}\big({\left.\kern-1.2pt{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\vphantom{\big|}\right|_{\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}}}\big)\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4}
, which implies
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
. Otherwise,
H^{1}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0
.

Proposition 17

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=(-\infty,a]
.
If
a\in[{\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2})
, then
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime},{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}
for
k=1
, and is zero otherwise. When
a\notin[{\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2})
, then
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})=0
for all
k
.

Proof

An identical argument as in the proof of Proposition
16
.

Theorem 10.4

Let
{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}
be a parametrized dynamical system conjugate to the above parametrization
{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}
of the S-shaped bifurcation. Then,

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}~\text{~ is acyclic and~}~H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{3}.

Moreover,
there exists
\lambda_{1},\lambda_{2}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a\in({\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2}]\\
0&\textnormal{otherwise.}\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty),

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a\in[{\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2})\\
0&\textnormal{otherwise.}\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=(-\infty,a].

Proof

Apply Theorem
8.1
, Proposition
14
, and Propositions
16
and
17
.

Remark 30

Note that the relative cohomologies
H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})
are the same for the trans-critical and S-shaped bifurcation.

Remark 31

If we consider the S-shaped bifurcation on an interval
X=I=[-c,c]
,
c\gg 1
, with time space
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}^{+}
and parameter space
\mathrm{\Lambda}=[-\lambda_{0},\lambda_{0}]
, with
\lambda_{0}=-c+c^{3}-\epsilon
,
0<\epsilon\ll 1
we obtain the following sheaf cohomology:

{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}}~\text{~ is acyclic and~}~H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}.

Moreover,
there exists a value
\lambda_{0}\in{\mathbb{R}}
such that

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a\in({\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2}]\\
0&\textnormal{otherwise.}\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty)\cap\mathrm{\Lambda},

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}}^{\bm{{\phi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a\in[{\lambda}_{1},{\lambda}_{2})\\
0&\textnormal{otherwise.}\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=(-\infty,a]\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}.

For free attractor sheaf we have:

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{2}&\textnormal{if }k=0\\
{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=1\\
0&\textnormal{if }k\geq 2\end{cases},

which is clearly not acyclic.

10.3
Comparing the attractor and free attractor sheaves

In the above treatment of the pitchfork, the saddle-node and transcritical bifurcations we have only used the attractor sheaf. We can reexamine the saddle-node bifurcation as studied in
Propositions
10
and
11
with the free attractor sheaf.
Let

{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X),

be the parametrized system for the saddle-node bifurcation as given in
10.1.2
,
where
\mathrm{\Lambda}={\mathbb{R}}
is parameter space,
{\mathbb{T}}={\mathbb{R}}
is the time space and
X
is the 2-point compactification of
{\mathbb{R}}
.

For the free attractor sheaf we have:

{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}~\text{~ is acyclic and ~}~H^{0}(\mathrm{\Lambda};{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4}.

Moreover,

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}^{4}&\textnormal{if }k=1\textnormal{ and }a>0\\
0&k\neq 1,\textnormal{or~}a\leq 0,\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=[a,\infty),

H^{k}(\mathrm{\Lambda},\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime};{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}})\cong\begin{cases}{\mathbb{Z}}_{2}&\textnormal{if }k=0\textnormal{ and }a<0\\
0&k\neq 0,\textnormal{or~}a\geq 0,\end{cases}\quad\text{with}\quad\mathrm{\Lambda}^{\prime}=(-\infty,a].

The abelian attractor sheaf, as shown in Theorem
10.4
, is acyclic for the S-shaped bifurcation. Proposition
15
demonstrates nontrivial trivial cohomology in dimension one for the free attractor sheaf. Consider the continuation of the union of both attracting fixed points, a section of both the free and abelian attractor sheaves on the interval between both two bifurcation points. Recall the ordered Čech complex on the open cover
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}=(-\infty,{\lambda}_{2})
and
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}=({\lambda}_{1},\infty)
:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1})\oplus{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathcal{A}\!\mathcal{t}\!\!\mathcal{t}}^{\bm{{\psi_{\bm{*}}}}}(\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}\cap\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2})}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces 0,}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\rho^{2}_{1,2}-\rho^{1}_{1,2}}

For both sheaves, this is not the restriction of any section from
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}
or
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}
. However, the symmetric Conley form lets us write this section as the sum of the sections corresponding to each attracting fixed point. Thus, for the abelian attractor sheaf, this gives a trivial cohomology class. For the free attractor sheaf the addition operation is formal, so we cannot write the union of the two attracting fixed points as a sum of sections from
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{1}
and
\mathrm{\Lambda}_{2}
.

Appendix B
Functorial properties of attractors

Proof
(Lemma
1
)

For
t\geq 0
, we have
\phi_{t}(h^{-1}(U))\subset(h^{-1}\circ h\circ\phi_{t})(h^{-1}(U))
. Since
h
is a quasiconjugacy, we have
(h^{-1}\circ h\circ\phi_{t})(h^{-1}(U))=h^{-1}(\psi^{\dagger}_{t}((h\circ h^{-1})(U)))\subset h^{-1}(\psi^{\dagger}_{t}(U))
and thus

\phi_{t}(h^{-1}(U))\subset h^{-1}(\psi^{\dagger}_{t}(U)),\quad\forall t\geq 0.

The inequality for
\omega
now follows from elementary properties of inverse images and closures:

\displaystyle\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(U))

\displaystyle=\bigcap_{t\geq 0}{\rm cl}\,\bigcup_{s\geq t}\phi_{s}\bigl(h^{-1}(U)\bigr)\subset\bigcap_{t\geq 0}{\rm cl}\,\bigcup_{s\geq t}h^{-1}\bigl(\psi^{\dagger}_{s}(U)\bigr)=\bigcap_{t\geq 0}{\rm cl}\,h^{-1}\Big(\bigcup_{s\geq t}\psi^{\dagger}_{s}(U)\Big)

\displaystyle\subset\bigcap_{t\geq 0}h^{-1}\Big({\rm cl}\,\bigcup_{s\geq t}\psi^{\dagger}_{s}(U)\Big)=h^{-1}\bigg(\bigcap_{t\geq 0}{\rm cl}\,\bigcup_{s\geq t}\psi^{\dagger}_{s}(U)\bigg)

\displaystyle=h^{-1}\bigg(\bigcap_{t\geq 0}{\rm cl}\,\bigcup_{x\in U}\bigcup_{\sigma\geq\tau(t,x)}\psi_{\sigma}(x)\bigg)=h^{-1}\bigg(\bigcap_{\tau\geq 0}{\rm cl}\,\bigcup_{\sigma\geq\tau}\psi_{\sigma}(U)\bigg)=h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(U)),

which uses the invertibility of the parametrization function
\tau
.
Finally applying
\omega_{\phi}
we obtain

\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(U))=\omega_{\phi}(\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(U)))\subset\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(U)))\subset\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(U))

so that

\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(U))=\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(U))),

(25)

which completes the proof.

Proof
(Remark
7
)

To deal with negative times we define
\tau(-t,x):=\tau(t,x)
in which case

\psi^{\dagger}_{-t}=\psi\bigl(\tau(-t,\cdot),\cdot\bigr)=\psi\bigl(-\tau(t,\cdot),\cdot\bigr)=\bigl(\psi^{\dagger}_{t}\bigr)^{-1}.

Let
x\in\phi_{-t}(h^{-1}(U))
so that
\phi_{t}(x)\in h^{-1}(U)
. Then, by the quasiconjugacy condition
h(\phi_{t}(x))=\psi^{\dagger}_{t}(h(x))\in U
, and therefore
h(x)\in\psi^{\dagger}_{-t}(U)
. This yields
x\in h^{-1}(\psi^{\dagger}_{-t}(U))
.
Summarizing we have

\phi_{-t}(h^{-1}(U))\subset h^{-1}(\psi^{\dagger}_{-t}(U)),\quad\forall t\geq 0.

The remainder of the proof is similar to the proof of Lemma
1
.

Proof
(Proposition
1
)

Since
A
is an attractor for
\psi
, there exists an attracting neighborhood
U
such that
\omega_{\psi}(U)=A
.
By Eqn. (
25
) we have

\omega_{\phi}\bigl(h^{-1}(U)\bigr)=\omega_{\phi}\bigl(h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(U))\bigr)=\omega_{\phi}\bigl(h^{-1}(A)\bigr),

which proves that
\omega_{\phi}\bigl(h^{-1}(A)\bigr)
is an attractor for
\phi
, since we already know
h^{-1}(U)
is an attracting neighborhood for
\phi
.

Therefore, for a quasiconjugacy
\tau\times h\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)
, the map
\omega_{\phi}\circ h^{-1}\colon{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
is well defined.
It remains to show that the latter is a lattice homomorphism. Preservation of joins is clear, cf. Property (v) for omega-limit sets. Let
A,A^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi)
, then

\displaystyle\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A\wedge A^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(A\cap A^{\prime})))\subset\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A\cap A^{\prime}))=\omega_{\phi}\bigl(h^{-1}(A)\cap h^{-1}(A^{\prime})\bigr)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\phi}\big(\omega_{\phi}\bigl(h^{-1}(A)\cap h^{-1}(A^{\prime})\bigr)\big)\subset\omega_{\phi}\big(\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A))\cap\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A^{\prime}))\big)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A))\wedge\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A^{\prime}))

Idempotency of
\omega_{\phi}
and Equation (
2
) imply

\displaystyle\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A))\wedge\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\omega_{\phi}\big(\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A))\cap\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A^{\prime}))\big)

\displaystyle\subset\omega_{\phi}\big(h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(A))\cap h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(A^{\prime}))\big)=\omega_{\phi}\big(h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(A)\cap\omega_{\psi}(A^{\prime}))\big)

\displaystyle=\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A)\cap h^{-1}(A^{\prime}))=\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A\cap A^{\prime}))

\displaystyle=\omega_{\phi}\big(\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A\cap A^{\prime}))\big)\subset\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(\omega_{\psi}(A\cap A^{\prime})))

\displaystyle=\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A\wedge A^{\prime}))),

which proves that

\omega_{\phi}\big(h^{-1}(A\wedge A^{\prime}))\big)=\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A))\wedge\omega_{\phi}(h^{-1}(A^{\prime})),

and thus

\omega_{\phi}\circ h^{-1}\colon{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi)\rightarrow{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
is a lattice homomorphism.

Proof
(Remark
6
)

If
\tau\times h\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\phi,\psi)
is a conjugacy, then

h\bigl(\phi_{t}(x)\bigr)=\psi_{t}^{\dagger}\bigl(h(x)\bigr).

(26)

Define
y=h(x)
and
s=\tau\bigl(t,h^{-1}(y)\bigr)
.
Since
h
is a homeomorphism, we obtain
\tau^{-1}(s,y)
, and therefore

\phi_{s}^{\dagger}\bigl(h^{-1}(y)\bigr)=h^{-1}\bigl(\psi_{s}(y)\bigr),

(27)

where
\phi_{s}^{\dagger}=\psi\bigl(\tau^{-1}(s,\cdot),\cdot\bigr)
.
This proves that
\tau^{-1}\times h^{-1}\in{\mathsf{hom}}(\psi,\phi)
is a conjugacy.

Let
A\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi)
, then by Proposition
1
, we have
\omega_{\phi}\bigl(h^{-1}(A)\bigr)\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
. By Equation (
27
) we have

\phi_{s}^{\dagger}\bigl(h^{-1}(A)\bigr)=h^{-1}\bigl(\psi_{s}(A)\bigr)=h^{-1}(A)
for all
s\geq 0
, which proves invariance of
h^{-1}(A)
. Furthermore, since
h
is a homeomorphism, it follows that
h^{-1}(A)
is closed, and thus

\omega_{\phi}\bigl(h^{-1}(A)\bigr)=h^{-1}(A)
, which proves that
h^{-1}(A)\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
.
Similarly,
h(A)\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi)
for all
A\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
.

Appendix C
Repellers

In Remarks
7
and
13
we indicated that one can also construct continuation frames
({\mathsf{Rep}},{\mathsf{RNbhd}},\alpha)
based repelling neighborhoods and repellers which yields the étalé space
\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Rep}}]
.
For a dynamical system
\phi\colon{\mathbb{T}}^{+}\times X\rightarrow X
we define
\phi_{-t}:=\phi_{t}^{-1}
as the inverse image. The map
\phi(-t,x)
also satisfies the semigroup property.
This allows us to define the notion
alpha-limit set
as

\alpha_{\phi}(U):=\bigcap_{t\geq 0}{\rm cl}\,\bigcup_{s\geq t}\phi_{-s}(U).

Some properties of
\alpha_{\phi}(U)
are: (i)
\alpha_{\phi}(U)
is compact, closed, (ii)
\alpha_{\phi}(U)
is a forward-backward invariant set for the dynamics, (iii)
\alpha_{\phi}\bigl(\alpha_{\phi}(U)\bigr)\supset\alpha_{\phi}(U)
,
(iv)
\alpha_{\phi}(U\cup V)=\alpha_{\phi}(U)\cup\alpha_{\phi}(V)
.
A neighborhood
U\subset X
is called a
repelling neighborhood
if
\alpha_{\phi}(U)\subset{\rm int\,}U
. Repelling neighborhoods form a bounded, distributive lattice denoted by
{\mathsf{RNbhd}}(\phi)
. The binary operations are
\cap
and
\cup
.
A subset
A\subset X
is called a
repeller
if there exists an repelling neighborhood
U\subset X
such that
R=\alpha_{\phi}(U)
, which is a neighborhood of
R
by definition. Repellers are compact, closed, forward-backward invariant sets and compose a bounded, distributive lattice
{\mathsf{Rep}}(\phi)
with binary operations
\cup
and
\cap
. As before

\phi\mapstochar\rightarrow{\mathsf{RNbhd}}(\phi)
and
\phi\mapstochar\rightarrow{\mathsf{Rep}}(\phi)
define the contravariant functors
{\mathsf{RNbhd}}
and
{\mathsf{Rep}}
from

{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
.
The functor
{\mathsf{RNbhd}}\colon{\mathbf{DS}}({\mathbb{T}},X)\rightarrow{\mathbf{BDLat}}
is a stable structure
and
({\mathsf{Rep}},{\mathsf{RNbhd}},\alpha)
forms a continuation frame in a similar way.
From the continuation frame
({\mathsf{Rep}},{\mathsf{RNbhd}},\alpha)
we obtain the étalé space

(\mathrm{\Pi}[{\mathsf{Rep}}],\pi)
.

For a dynamical system
\phi
consider the duality isomorphism
A\mapstochar\rightarrow A^{*}
,
A\in{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)
.
Since for
U\in{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)
the maps

U\mapstochar\rightarrow U^{c}
and
\omega_{\phi}(U)\mapstochar\rightarrow\alpha_{\phi}(U^{c})
define lattice isomorphisms we also have the natural transformations
{}^{c}\colon{\mathsf{ANbhd}}\Longleftrightarrow{\mathsf{RNbhd}}
and
{}^{*}\colon{\mathsf{Att}}\Longleftrightarrow{\mathsf{Rep}}
. This yields the following commutative diagram:

{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\psi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{RNbhd}}(\psi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{ANbhd}}(\phi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{RNbhd}}(\phi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Att}}(\psi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Rep}}(\psi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Att}}(\phi)}
{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Rep}}(\phi)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces h^{-1}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces c}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\omega_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces\alpha_{\phi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*_{\psi}}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Att}}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces{\mathsf{Rep}}(h)}
\scriptstyle{\lx@inpgf@ignorespaces*_{\phi}}

where
{\mathsf{Att}}(h)=\omega_{\phi}\circ h^{-1}
and
{\mathsf{Rep}}(f):=*_{\phi}\circ\omega_{\phi}\circ h^{-1}\circ*_{\psi}
.
This asymmetry between attractors and repellers is typical for noninvertible systems.
For invertible systems the symmetry is restored so that
{\mathsf{Rep}}(h)=\alpha_{\phi}\circ h^{-1}
.

Appendix D
Function spaces and the compact-open topology

We recall some basic facts about topologies on function spaces of continuous functions.
Let
X
and
Y
be arbitrary topological spaces and let
C(X,Y)
the denote the set of all continuous maps
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
. A topology on
C(X,Y)
which is of particular importance is the
compact-open topology
which is defined a
subbasis of sets of the form

O(K,U):=\bigl\{f~|~f(K)\subset U\hbox{ for $K$ compact in $X$ and $U$ open in $Y$}\bigr\},

where
K
ranges over all compact subsets in
X
and
U
ranges over all open subsets in
Y
, cf.
Fox
.
If
X
is a locally compact, Hausdorff space then the compact-open topology is the weakest topology such that the map
(f,x)\mapstochar\rightarrow f(x)
,
f\in C(X,Y)
, is continuous, cf.
(
Marcelo
, Cor. 1.2.4)
.
If
X
is compact and
Y
is a metric space with metric
d
, then the compact-open topology corresponds with the metric topology on
C(X,Y)
given by the metric:

d(f,g)=\sup\limits_{x\in X}d(f(x),g(x)),\qquad f,g\in C(X,Y),

cf.
Muger
;
Schwendiman
.

Let
\mathrm{\Lambda}
be an arbitrary topological space. For a continuous map
h\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\times X\rightarrow Y
we define the
transpose
of
h
by:

h_{\bm{*}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow C(X,Y),\quad\quad\lambda\mapstochar\rightarrow h_{\bm{*}}(\lambda)=h^{\lambda}:=h(\lambda,\cdot).

Following the terminology in
Escardo
we say that
a topology on
C(X,Y)
is
weak
if continuity of
h
implies continuity of the transpose
h_{\bm{*}}
, and a topology is
strong
if continuity of the transpose
h_{\bm{*}}
implies continuity of
h
. For arbitrary topological spaces
X,Y
and
\mathrm{\Lambda}
the compact-open topology is a weak topology on
C(X,Y)
, i.e.
h
continuous implies that
h_{\bm{*}}
is continuous, cf.
(
Fox
, Lemma 1)
,
Muger
.
If
X
is regular and locally compact (in particular for locally compact, Hausdorff spaces),
then the compact-open topology is is both weak and strong, i.e.
h
is continuous if and only if
h_{\bm{*}}
is continuous, cf.
(
Fox
, Theorem 1)
,
Muger
. This implies that for regular and locally compact spaces
X

the compact-open topology on
C(X,Y)
is both weak and strong, which is also referred to as an
exponential topology
, cf.
Escardo
.
The latter is unique.
Finally, the map
h\mapstochar\rightarrow h_{\bm{*}}
is an embedding when both
\mathrm{\Lambda}
and
X
are Hausdorff spaces. The map is a homeomorphism when
\mathrm{\Lambda}
is Hausdorff and
X
is locally compact, Hausdorff, cf.
Muger
.

For a compact metric space
(X,d)
define
\big({\mathsf{H}}(X),d_{\mathsf{H}}\big)
to be the metric space of compact subsets of
X
equipped with the Hausdorff metric
d_{\mathsf{H}}
.
Every continuous function
f\colon X\rightarrow Y
induces a continuous function
f^{\mathsf{H}}\colon{\mathsf{H}}(X)\rightarrow{\mathsf{H}}(Y)
, which sends compact subsets to their image under the function
f
.
Recall the Hausdorff metric:

d_{\mathsf{H}}(K,K^{\prime}):=\max\bigg\{\sup_{x\in K}\inf_{x^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}}d(x,x^{\prime}),\sup_{x^{\prime}\in K^{\prime}}\inf_{x\in K}d(x,x^{\prime})\bigg\},\quad K,K^{\prime}\in{\mathsf{H}}(X).

Lemma 18

Let
X
,
Y
be compact metric spaces,
\mathrm{\Lambda}
a topological space, and
h\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\times X\rightarrow Y
continuous map. Then, the function

h^{\mathsf{H}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\times{\mathsf{H}}(X)\rightarrow{\mathsf{H}}(Y)\quad\quad(\lambda,K)\mapstochar\rightarrow h^{\mathsf{H}}\big(\{\lambda\}\times K\big)

is continuous.

Proof

We will first prove the assignment

{\mathsf{D}}\colon C(X,Y)\rightarrow C({\mathsf{H}}(X),{\mathsf{H}}(Y))\quad\quad f\mapstochar\rightarrow{\mathsf{D}}(f):=f^{\mathsf{H}},

is continuous. Let
f,g\in C(X,Y)
. Then,
d_{C}(f,g)=\sup\limits_{x\in X}d(f(x),g(x))
and

d_{C_{\mathsf{H}}}(f^{\mathsf{H}},g^{\mathsf{H}})=\sup\limits_{K\in{\mathsf{H}}(X)}d_{\mathsf{H}}\big(f(K),g(K)\big).

Since
d(y,y^{\prime})\leq\sup\limits_{x\in X}d(f(x),g(x))=d(f,g)
for any choice of
y\in f(K),y^{\prime}\in g(K)
it follows that
d_{C_{\mathsf{H}}}(f^{\mathsf{H}},g^{\mathsf{H}})\leq d_{C}(f,g)
. Moreover, since points are compact subsets, the reversed inequality holds as well:

d_{C}(f,g)=\sup\limits_{x\in X}d_{\mathsf{H}}\big(f(\{x\}),g(\{x\})\big)\leq d_{C_{\mathsf{H}}}(f^{\mathsf{H}},g^{\mathsf{H}}),

which proves that
{\mathsf{D}}
is an isometry implying its continuity.
The metric topology on
C({\mathsf{H}}(X),{\mathsf{H}}(Y))
coincides with the compact-open topology and therefore

h_{\mathsf{H}}
is continuous if and only if its
transpose

h^{\mathsf{h}}_{\bm{*}}
, given by

h^{\mathsf{H}}_{\bm{*}}\colon\mathrm{\Lambda}\rightarrow C\big({\mathsf{H}}(X),{\mathsf{H}}(Y)\big),\quad\quad\lambda\mapstochar\rightarrow h^{\mathsf{H}}_{\bm{*}}(\lambda)=h_{\mathsf{H}}^{\lambda}:=h_{\mathsf{H}}\big(\{\lambda\},\cdot\big),

is continuous,
Fox
;
Escardo
.
Note that
h^{\mathsf{H}}_{\bm{*}}={\mathsf{D}}\circ h_{\bm{*}}
which proves that
h^{\mathsf{H}}_{*}
is continuous, which completes the proof.

Remark 32

In this paper we abuse notation by writing
h(K)
,
K\in{\mathsf{H}}(X)
denoting
h^{\mathsf{H}}(K)
in accordance with the analogous notation for
h(U)=\{y=h(x)~|~x\in U\}
,
U\subset X
.
